# Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas by Purboshri Borpuzari


> **TL;DR:** This scholarly research monograph by **Purboshri Borpuzari** provides an in-depth academic analysis of classical Sanskrit medical literature, historical clinical practices, and traditional Ayurvedic principles. It offers critical insights into ancient medical ethics, pharmacology, and therapeutic methodologies, establishing an evidence-based link between classical literature and modern health science.

---

**Author / Source:** by Purboshri Borpuzari


**Total Chapters/Sections:** 120

## 1. Acknowledgement


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Acknowledgement’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

## 2. Part 1 - The Meaning and Scope of the word Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Meaning and Scope of the word Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 1 - The Meaning and Scope of the word Purāṇa

The Purāṇas are an unfailing source of information that helps us to reconstruct the history of Indian culture and civilization. By culture and civilization we mean the history of Indian polity, of Indian society, the history of religion and philosophy, legal history, the history of arts and crafts, architecture and iconography besides royal dynasties and the period of their rule.[1] The only species of Indian literature, which can claim, next to the Vedas, to reach back to great antiquity, is the Purāṇa.[2] Long before the beginning of the Christian era it came also to be used as the designation of a class of books dealing, among other matters, with old-world stories and legends.[3] The word purāṇa means old story (purāṇam ākhyānam).[4] The Śabdakalpadruma and the

Vācaspatyam explain Purāṇa as that which remains in the past and deals with the past.[5] Purāṇas are the old narratives or ancient legends having informative contents.

Yāska in his Nirukta defines Purāṇa as–

purā navaṃ bhavati[6]
i.e. it was new in the days of yore.

purā navaṃ bhavati[6]

i.e. it was new in the days of yore.

Sāyaṇācārya in the Ṛgveda[7] explains the word purāṇyā as—

purā kṛtayā.

purā kṛtayā.

So the term purāṇa indicates ‘ancient one’ according to this explanation.

The etymology of the term purāṇa is furnished by the Vāyu Purāṇa as follows—

yasmāt purā hyanatīdaṃ purāṇaṃ tena tat smṛtam[8]
i.e. that which lives from very remote times.

yasmāt purā hyanatīdaṃ purāṇaṃ tena tat smṛtam[8]

i.e. that which lives from very remote times.

The Purāṇas have their roots in the Vedic literature; many a legend, from the Ṛgvedic hymns and from the Brāhmaṇas, reappears in the Purāṇas.[9] They are thus composed with a view to get the knowledge revealed in the Vedas through stories. The Brāhmaṇa, who may know the four Vedas with the Aṅgas and Upaniṣads, should not really be regarded as having attained proficiency, if he should not thoroughly know the Purāṇa. He should reinforce the Veda with the Itihāsa and Purāṇa.[10]

The majority of people act according to the popular rituals which are recorded in the Purāṇas. The Purāṇic tradition is a combination of the Vedic ritualistic and popular mythical traditions.

That is why the Purāṇa is regarded as the fifth Veda in addition to the four Vedas i.e., the Ṛgveda, the Yajurveda, the Sāmaveda and the Atharvaveda—

purāṇaṃ pañcamo vedaḥ.[11]

purāṇaṃ pañcamo vedaḥ.[11]

The Chāndogya Upaniṣad too mentions the Itihāsa-Purāṇa as the fifth Veda—

itihāsapurāṇaṃ pañcamaṃ vedānām.[12]

itihāsapurāṇaṃ pañcamaṃ vedānām.[12]

The Skanda Purāṇa states that the Purāṇas are not inferior to the Vedas. The Purāṇas are as steady as the Vedas. All the Vedas are firmly rooted in the Purāṇas. It opines that whatever is not seen in the Vedas or Smṛtis, is seen in the Purāṇas.[13] The Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa mentions Itihāsa and Purāṇa as subjects of study by a householder. It has been mentioned in the context of revelation on Pañcamahāyajña.[14] The Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa opines that it is called Purāṇa because it was in olden times.[15]

The Śabdakalpadruma[16] too contains the same meaning—

purā purvasmin kāle bhava iti
i.e., which was in ancient times.

purā purvasmin kāle bhava iti

i.e., which was in ancient times.

The Matsya Purāṇa gives the definition of the word purāṇa as—

purātanasya kalpasya purāṇāni vidurbudhāḥ.[17]
‘containing the records of past events’

purātanasya kalpasya purāṇāni vidurbudhāḥ.[17]

‘containing the records of past events’

The Padma Purāṇa says that it is called Purāṇa because it is interested in the past and therefore, it describes about the past.[18] Amarasiṃha in the Amarakoṣa describes Purāṇa as ancient tales.[19]

The Purāṇas have been utilized through centuries not only for educating the mass mind and infusing it with the nobler ideas of life but also for tactfully solving the religious, social and economic problems which were created in ancient India by the rise of various religious movements and the repeated invasions made by foreign races.[20] These works afford us, more than other works of the time a great insight into all phases and aspects of Hinduism.[21] For the modern Hinduism, the Purāṇas are religious treatises of divine origin. Hence, they have been a great source of inspiration to the mass-mind, especially, on religious matter.[22] That is why, the Purāṇas ought to have more careful study than has previously been dedicated to them.

## 4. Part 2 - Characteristics of the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Characteristics of the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 2 - Characteristics of the Purāṇas

The Matsya Purāṇa and the Vāyu Purāṇa speak of five characteristics to distinguish a Purāṇa.[1] They are as follows—

Creation (sarga),

Dissolution and re-creation (pratisarga),

The lineage line (vaṃśa),

Jurisdiction of the fourteen Manus, belonging to the solar and lunar races (manvantara), and

The genealogy of the kings of the solar and lunar races (vaṃśyānucarita).

The Purāṇas, with these five characteristics, sing the glory of Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Arka and Rudra.They also describe the creation and dissolution of the Earth.[2] Moreover, the Purāṇas describe about the four Vargas i.e. Dharma, Artha, Kāma and Mokṣa.[3]

Amarasiṃha also describes the five characteristics of the Purāṇas in the Amarakoṣa.[4] The Agni Purāṇa too mentions the five characteristics.[5] The five fold characteristics of Purāṇas are also mentioned by some other Purāṇas such as the Śiva Purāṇa, Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa, Viṣṇu Purāṇa, Skanda Purāṇa, Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa and the Kūrma Purāṇa.[6]

But the Bhāgavata Purāṇa mentions the ten characteristics of Purāṇas as described by the Brāhmaṇa sages in conformity to Vedas and Śāstras.[7] It has also elaborated all the characteristics of the Purāṇas thoroughly.

They are as follows—

1. Sarga (subtle creation)

According to the Bhāgavata Purāṇa, sarga consists of the growth of mahat from the interruption in the balance of stability of the three guṇas of the prakṛti,leading to the development of three-fold ahaṃkāra (sāttvika, rājasa and tāmasa),the evolutes of which are subtle elements (bhūtas), the sense organs and their objects(gross elements and the deities presiding over the sense organs).[8]

2. Visarga (gross creation)

Visarga is the creation of the gross mobile and immobile nature.[9]

3. Vṛtti (sustenance)

The immobile creation(food-grains, fruits, roots) and some products of mobile creation too form the Vṛtti (sustenance) of the mobile creation. In case of human beings the Vṛtti is determined by their character, wish and scriptural commands.[10]

4 Rakṣā (protection of the universe)

Rakṣā consists of the incarnations and the sportive acts of Lord Viṣṇu, from age to age appearing among sub-human beings, human beings, sages and among gods, and the annihilation of the haters of the Vedas.[11]

5 Hetu (cause or the lust for Karmas)

Hetu of the universe is Jīva (individual soul). He is the doer of actions under the influence of ignorance.[12]

6 Antara (the periods of Manu)

Manvantara(Manu-period) is characterized by six special aspects. They are the period presided over by the particular Manu, the set of gods, the sons of Manu, Indra, seven sages and the incarnations of Hari.[13]

7 Vaṃśa (dynasties of kings)

Vaṃśa is the race of kings who were born of god Brahmā and continued throughout the three divisions of time–the past, present and future.[14]

8 Vaṃśānucarita(history of the kings)

Vaṃśānucarita is the history of the kings and their descendants.[15]

9 Saṃsthā (physical annihilation)

The destruction of the universe created by Māyā is called Saṃsthā. This destruction is of four types-Naimittika(Occasional or brought about by a particular cause), Prākṛtika(of the Prakṛti), Nitya(Constant) and Ātyantika(Absolute).[16]

10 Apāśraya (ultimate reality)

Apāśraya is the highest goal of the characteristics of the Purāṇas. Apāśraya (the last support or ultimate reality) is Brahman. It is depicted as a observer to what is caused by Māyā, as in the three phases of Jīva(viz. wakefulness, dreaming and dreamless deep sleep) and is also somewhat different from them as in Samādhi.[17]

The Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa also describes the ten main characteristics of the Purāṇas—

sṛṣṭiścāpi visṛṣṭiśca sthitisteṣāṃ ca pālanam/ 
karmaṇāṃ vāsanā vārtā manūnāṃ cākrameṇa ca// 
varṇanaṃ pralayānāṃ ca mokṣasya ca nirūpaṇam/ 
tatkīrtanaṃ harereva vedānāṃ ca pṛthak pṛthak//

sṛṣṭiścāpi visṛṣṭiśca sthitisteṣāṃ ca pālanam/ 
karmaṇāṃ vāsanā vārtā manūnāṃ cākrameṇa ca// 
varṇanaṃ pralayānāṃ ca mokṣasya ca nirūpaṇam/ 
tatkīrtanaṃ harereva vedānāṃ ca pṛthak pṛthak//

These are as follows—

sṛṣṭi (original creation),

visṛṣṭi (creation of other movable and immovable things from mūla prakṛti),

sthiti (period of life),

pālana (defense),

karma vāsanā (necessity which makes a man liable for his acts or the desire for work),

manuvārtā (information about different Manus),

7. pralaya-varṇanā (the account relating to the eternal dissolution of the world),

mokṣa-nirūpaṇa (the determination of the nature of redemption),

harikīrtana (the eulogies of Hari) and

vedakīrtana (reciting of the Vedas separately).[18]

This Purāṇa further mentions that the Mahāpurāṇas are to deal with these tenfold characteristic.[19]

The Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa mentions the five-fold feature too. But according to this Purāṇa, these are appropriate for the Upapurāṇas only.[20]

The Padma Purāṇa[21] looks the various Purāṇas as the various limbs of Viṣṇu. the Padma Purāṇa is said to be Viṣṇu himself,—

the Brahma Purāṇa being the head,

the Padma Purāṇa the heart,

the Viṣṇu Purāṇa the right arm,

the Śiva Purāṇa being the left arm,

the Bhāgavata Purāṇa the two thighs,

the Nāradīya Purāṇa the navel,

the Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa the right shank,

the Agni Purāṇa being the left shank,

the Bhaviṣya Purāṇa the right knee,

the Brahmavaivarta being the left one,

the Liṅga Purāṇa the right ankle,

the Varāha Purāṇa being the left,

the Skanda Purāṇa the hair in the body,

the Vāmana Purāṇa the skin,

the Kūrma Purāṇa the back,

the Matsya Purāṇa the fat,

the Garuḍa Purāṇa being the marrow and

the Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa the bones.

## 5. Preface


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Preface’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Preface

Since the ancient period, Agriculture and allied activities have been providing livelihood for most of the population. Agriculture and Animal Husbandry can neither be regarded as an industry, nor a means to make profit. In fact Agriculture and Animal Husbandry is the mainstay of living beings. For at least the last 4500 years the Indian society has been primarily an Agricultural one. The ancient practice of Agriculture and Animal Husbandry are still very much popular. Even in the 21st century this ancient practice is much more relevant. Among the ancient treatises, the Vedas and the Purāṇas may be regarded as the source of inspiration for the practice of Agriculture and Animal Husbandry in India.

Right from the days of doing my post-graduation in Sanskrit, I had a keen desire for doing research on such an uncommon topic which has relevance even in this era of science and technology. From then on, when I moved towards my revered teacher Dr. Nalini Devi Misra, Professor, Gauhati University, she guided me to select the topic and encouraged me to go for the same. The Purāṇas were selected to do research with, because it was noticed that regarding Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Vedas, a lot of books are available. But the Purāṇas are seen having no specific books on the subjects cited above. Moreover, the Purāṇic records regarding Agriculture and Animal husbandry are scattered here and there. Therefore, a keen interest developed in me for doing a detailed and systematic study covering the major Purāṇas and thus, a modest attempt has been made to set up a thesis in five chapters on the topic entitled–Agriculture and Animal Husbandry in the Purāṇas—A Study, for the degree of Ph.D. of the University of Gauhati. But due to the limitation of space and time all the eighteen major Purāṇas are not included in my dissertation. Only five major Purāṇas have been covered mainly. They are the Agni Purāṇa, Bhāgavata Purāṇa, Matsya Purāṇa, Vāyu Purāṇa, and Viṣṇu Purāṇa. For this systematic study, various books on Agriculture and Animal Husbandry have helped me a lot. I have taken the design of arranging the methods orderly from those books, but the investigation is purely mine. The less data the Purāṇas have regarding Agriculture have been incorporated in the second chapter and Animal Husbandry has been included in the fourth chapter. It is hoped that this humble effort will be of some interests for the lovers of Sanskrit literature with regard to the role of the Purāṇas in the field of Agriculture and Animal Husbandry.

With these prefatory words, I with all modesty and sincerity put forward this work to the learned world. It is the outcome of my academic quest for last six years under the supervision of my revered preceptor, Prof. Nalini Devi Misra. I shall consider rewarded if this work can arise some curiosities in the minds of the readers.

## 6. Part 3 - Authorship of the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Authorship of the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 3 - Authorship of the Purāṇas

The Purāṇas contain various references regarding their authorship. According to the Matsya Purāṇa, Vyāsa, the son of Satyavatī is the author of all the eighteen Purāṇas.[1] It states that Vyāsa has reduced the sense of the Vedas in the Purāṇas.

Vyāsa, after accumulating the eighteen Purāṇas, indulged in the composition of the Mahābhārata, with a view to enlarge the meaning of Vedas, which was an addition to the Purāṇas.[2] The Vāyu Purāṇa also mentions that Vyāsa, the son of Parāśara has composed the Paurāṇikī kathā(ancient chronicle story).[3] The Matsya Purāṇa[4] and the Nāradīya Purāṇa[5] opines that in the beginning there was only one Purāṇa, that was written by Vyāsa.[6] Vyāsa, after dividing the single Purāṇa, containing four lakhs of ślokas, into eighteen Purāṇas, spread them in the world.[7]

The Vāyu Purāṇa[8], the Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa[9] and the Viṣṇu Purāṇa[10] give an account, how the original Purāṇa came into existence. According to those three Purāṇas Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana divided the single Veda into four and arranged them, and thus, he was called Vyāsa. He handed them over to his four disciples, one to each, namely Paila, Vaiśampāyana, Jaimini and Sumantu. Then with tales (ākhyāna), anecdotes(upākhyāna), songs(gāthā) and lore (kalpajokti) that had commentary down from the ages, he compiled a Purāṇa,[11] and taught it and the Itihāsa to his fifth disciple, the Sūta Romaharṣaṇa or Lomaharṣaṇa.[12]

Romaharṣaṇa made that Purāṇa-Saṃhitā into six versions and taught them to his six disciples,—

Ātreya Sumati,

Kāśyapa Akṛtavraṇa,

Bharadvāja Agnivarcas,

Vasiṣṭha Mitrayu,

Sāvarṇi Somadatti, and

Suśarman Śāṃśapāyana.

Three of them, Kāśyapa, Sāvarṇi and Śāṃśapāyana, made three separate Saṃhitās, which were called by their names. Romaharṣaṇa's Saṃhitā and those three were the root-compositions (mūla-Saṃhitā). They consisted of four divisions (pāda). Excepting Śāṃśapāyana's Saṃhitā, each contained four thousand verses.[13]

After the composition of the original Purāṇa, by Vyāsa, his disciple Romaharṣaṇa taught it to his son Ugrasravas.[14] Ugrasravas, the Sauti, appears as the reciter in some of the extant Purāṇas.[15]

The Bhāgavata Purāṇa mentions six persons to be traditionally noted as the teachers(paurāṇika) of the Purāṇas. They are—

Trayyāruṇi,

Kaśyapa,

Sāvarṇi,

Akṛtavraṇa,

Vaiśampāyana and

Hārita.[16]

## 7. Part 5 - Number of the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Number of the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 5 - Number of the Purāṇas

The Mahāpurāṇas are held to be eighteen in number. Almost in all the Purāṇas, the list of eighteen Mahāpurāṇas, is found.[1]

In the Matsya Purāṇa[2], the Agni Purāṇa,[3] and the Nārada Purāṇa[4] the list of the eighteen Purāṇas is as follows:

Brahma-purāṇa;

Padma-purāṇa;

Viṣṇu-purāṇa;

Vāyu-purāṇa;

Bhāgavata-purāṇa;

Nāradīya-purāṇa;

Mārkaṇḍeya-purāṇa;

Agni-purāṇa;

Bhaviṣya-purāṇa;

Brahmavaivarta-purāṇa;

Liṅga-purāṇa;

Varāha-purāṇa;

Skanda-purāṇa;

Vāmana-purāṇa;

Kūrma-purāṇa;

Matsya-purāṇa;

Garuḍa-purāṇa and;

Brahmāṇḍa-purāṇa.;

In the Matsya Purāṇa,the Agni Purāṇa, the Nārada Purāṇa and in the Skanda Purāṇa,[5] Vāyavīya occurs fourth in the serial order whereas the Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa[6] the Viṣṇu Purāṇa[7] and the Bhāgavata Purāṇa[8] mentions the Śiva Purāṇa instead of the Vāyu Purāṇa. The Śaiva of the lists cannot be other than the extant Vāyu, which claims to be a Śaivite Purāṇa. The name Vāyavīya may have commentary into vogue much earlier because even the Mahābhārata mentions a Purāṇa  proclaimed by Vāyu, and the name Śaiva may have been originated at a later stage in view of its Śaivite tendencies.[9] The Skanda Purāṇa mentions that as the fourth Purāṇa was originally proclaimed by Vāyu, so this Purāṇa is remembered as Vāyu Purāṇa. And since devotion to Śiva has been propounded in it, so the other name for this Purāṇa is Śiva Purāṇa.[10]

In the Bhāgavata Purāṇa the names of the eighteen Purāṇas is serialized differently—

brāhmaṃ pādmaṃ vaiṣṇavaṃca śaivaṃ laiṅgaṃ sagāruḍam/
nāradīyaṃ bhāgavatamāgneyaṃ skāndasaṃjñitam//
bhaviṣyaṃ brahmavaivarttaṃ mārkaṇḍeyaṃ savāmanam/
vārāhaṃ mātsyaṃ kaurmaṃca brahmāṇḍākhyamiti triṣaṭ//[11]

brāhmaṃ pādmaṃ vaiṣṇavaṃca śaivaṃ laiṅgaṃ sagāruḍam/
nāradīyaṃ bhāgavatamāgneyaṃ skāndasaṃjñitam//
bhaviṣyaṃ brahmavaivarttaṃ mārkaṇḍeyaṃ savāmanam/
vārāhaṃ mātsyaṃ kaurmaṃca brahmāṇḍākhyamiti triṣaṭ//[11]

The Vāyu Purāṇa[12] too gives the list of the Purāṇas in a different order as follows—

Matsya-purāṇa;

Bhaviṣya-purāṇa;

Mārkaṇḍeya-purāṇa;

Brahmavaivarta-purāṇa;

Brahmāṇḍa-purāṇa;

Bhāgavata-purāṇa;

Brahma-purāṇa;

Vāmana-purāṇa,;

Ādika-purāṇa;

Anila-purāṇa (Vāyu-purāṇa);

Nāradīya-purāṇa;

Vainateya-purāṇa;

Padma-purāṇa;

Kūrma-purāṇa;

Saukara-purāṇa and;

Skanda-purāṇa.

In the Vāyu Purāṇa, the names of only sixteen Purāṇas are given although ‘eighteen Purāṇas’ are spoken with reference to.[13] And instead of the terms Garuḍa and Varāha, Vainateya and Saukara are used.

The Devi Bhāgavata Purāṇa has stated the following sonnet for memorizing the names of the Purāṇas easily—

madvayaṃ bhadvayaṃ caiva bratrayaṃ vacatuṣṭayam/
anāpaliṃ gakūskāni purāṇāni pṛthakpṛthak//[14]

madvayaṃ bhadvayaṃ caiva bratrayaṃ vacatuṣṭayam/
anāpaliṃ gakūskāni purāṇāni pṛthakpṛthak//[14]

Here, madvayam refers to the two Purāṇas the names of which begin with ma. They are: Mārkaṇḍeya and Matsya. Similarly the word bhadvayaṃ signifies the two Purāṇas, the names of which begin with bha. They are: Bhāgavata and Bhaviṣya. The names of the other Purāṇas are to be memorized similarly.

The numbers of verses of the eighteen Purāṇas are also mentioned in the Purāṇas.[15] According to the Skanda Purāṇa[16] the number of verses in the various Purāṇas are—

Brahma: 10000 verses;

Padma: 55000 verses;

Viṣṇu: 23000 verses;

Vāyu: 24000 verses;

Bhaviṣya: 14500 verses;

Mārkaṇḍeya: 9000 verses;

Agni: 16000 verses;

Nāradīya: 25000 verses;

Bhāgavata: 18000 verses;

Brahmavaivarta: 18000 verses;

Liṅga: 11000 verses;

Varāha: 24000 verses;

Skanda: 81000 verses;

Vāmana: 10000 verses;

Kūrma: 17000 verses;

Matsya: 14000 verses;

Garuḍa: 19000 verses and

Brahmāṇḍa: 12800 verses.

## 8. Part 4 - Date of the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Date of the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 4 - Date of the Purāṇas

Regarding the date of the Purāṇas nothing can be said certainly. The Atharvaveda says that the Purāṇas were originated from the residue (ucchiṣṭa) of the sacrifice.[1] The Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad assigns the origin of the Purāṇas to the breath of the Mahābhūta (the Great Being, Paramātman, the Supreme Soul).[2] According to the Padma Purāṇa, the Purāṇas are prior to the Vedas too.[3] The Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa too opines that the origin of the Purāṇas is pre-Vedic.[4] According to the Vāyu Purāṇa and the Matsya Purāṇa, it was Brahmā who at the very beginning remembered the Purāṇas among all the śāstras and then uttered the four Vedas from his mouths. So, the Purāṇas are considered the first among the śāstras.[5] And for this reason, the Purāṇas appear to claim themselves to be equal to the Vedas.[6]

The Agni Purāṇa says that Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana Vyāsa divided the Vedas into different śākhās or groups. Vyāsa also composed the Purāṇas and the Itihāsas. The six sages such as Sumati, Agnivarcā, Mitrayu, Śāṃśapāyana, Kṛtavrata and Sāvarṇi, who were the disciples of Sūta Lomaharṣaṇa, then received the Purāṇas from Vyāsa. After that these sages composed Saṃhitās of the eighteen Purāṇas such as Brahma etc.[7] So according to this reference, the Purāṇas are contemporary with the Vedas. Pargiter, in his Ancient Indian Historical Tradition, places the Purāṇas in the same chronological rank with the Vedas.[8] Again, as the Arthaśāstra[9] of Kauṭilya and the Manusmṛti[10] of the sage Manu also refers to the Purāṇas, so it can be assumed that the Purāṇas were earlier to the Arthaśāstra and Manusmṛti.

F.E. Pargiter opines that the Purāṇas began long before Āpastamba's time. His sūtra work is estimated by Bühler as not later than the third century B.C., and possibly 150-200 years earlier. Hence, the Purāṇas must have existed at least as early as the beginning of the fifth century B.C.[11] Winternitz says that the earlier Purāṇas must have commentary into being before the 7th century.[12] Again, discovery of a Bengal Manuscript of the Skanda Purāṇa, written in Gupta script, shows that this Purāṇa was in existence in the 7th Century A.D.[13] Kumārila Bhaṭṭa, the philosopher, (about 750 A.D) takes the Purāṇas as sources of law, while Śaṃkarācārya (9th century A.D) and Rāmānuja (12th century A.D) quote the Purāṇas as holy texts.[14] R.C. Hazra in his Studies on the Purāṇic Records of Hindu Rites and Customs mentions that as the Mahābhārata and the Harivaṃśa mentions about the eighteen Purāṇas, so it can only just be doubted that the Purāṇas were originated not later than the first quarter of the seventh century A.D.[15]

According to Pargiter, the Vāyu Purāṇa existed before A.D. 620, because it is referred to by poet Bāṇabhaṭṭa in his historical novel Harṣa-carita, and the writing in a Manuscript of the Skanda in the Royal Library of Nepal shows that that Purāṇa also existed about that time.[16] V.R.R. Dikshitar in his Some Aspects of the Vāyu Purāṇa opines that the epic Mahābhārata and its supplement the Harivaṃśa quote the Vāyu Purāṇa. This shows that this Purāṇa is much anterior to the epic Mahābhārata.[17] Again, the origin of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa may be assigned to the 13th century according to Colebrooke, Burnouf and Wilson.[18] Regarding the composition of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa it is stated that this Purāṇa was composed most probably in the last quarter of the third or the first quarter of the fourth century A.D.[19] The Agni Purāṇa is said to have been composed during the ninth century.[20] Regarding the date of the Matsya Purāṇa, Pargiter in his The Purāṇa Text of the Dynasties of the Kali Age opines that the compilation of the Matsya Purāṇa was begun in the latter part of the 2nd century in the reign of the Andhra king Yajña-Śrī Sātakarṇi.[21] A.M.T. Jackson thinks that Megasthenes was acquainted with part, at least, of a Purāṇic cosmogony and with a Purāṇic list of kings and also with a Purāṇic description of the upper and the lower world.[22] Observing Jackson’s point of view S.D. Gyani opines that Megasthenes had before him the same Purāṇa that has already been shown to have existed from the Vedic times to the 2nd Century B.C., the contents of which are, more or less, reproduced in the more primitive parts of the existing Purāṇas. The earliest part of the Purāṇas therefore, was compiled not later than the last quarter of the 4th century B.C.[23]

## 9. Part 6 - Description of the Upapurāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Description of the Upapuranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 6 - Description of the Upapurāṇas

The Upapurāṇas too are said to be eighteen.[1] According to the Kūrma Purāṇa, the various Upapurāṇas are—

Ādya-purāṇa (Sanatkumārokta),

Nārasiṃha-purāṇa,

Skānda-purāṇa,

Śivadharma-purāṇa,

Durvāsāsokta-purāṇa (as stated by the sage Durvāsas),

Nāradīya-purāṇa,

Kāpila-purāṇa,

Vāmana-purāṇa,

Uśanaserita-purāṇa (as stated by the sage Uśanas),

Brahmāṇḍa-purāṇa,

Vāruṇa-purāṇa,

Kālikā-purāṇa,

Māheśvara-purāṇa,

Śāmba-purāṇa,

Saura-purāṇa,

Parāśarokta-purāṇa (as stated by the sage Parāśara),

Mārīca-purāṇa, and

Bhārgava-purāṇa.[2]

The Bṛhaddharma Purāṇa also contains a list having some similar and some different names from the list of Kūrma Purāṇa. These are—

Ādi-purāṇa,

Āditya-purāṇa,

Bṛhannāradīya-purāṇa,

Nāradīya-purāṇa,

Nandīśvara-purāṇa,

Bṛhannandīśvara-purāṇa,

Śāmba-purāṇa,

Kriyāyogasāra-purāṇa,

Kālikā-purāṇa,

Dharma-purāṇa,

Viṣṇudharmottara-purāṇa,

Śivadharma-purāṇa,

Viṣṇudharma-purāṇa,

Vāmana-purāṇa,

Vāruṇa-purāṇa,

Nārasiṃha-purāṇa,

Bhārgava-purāṇa and

Bṛhaddharma-purāṇa.[3]

The Matsya Purāṇa again mentions four Upapurāṇas. They are as follows:

a) Nārasiṃha Upapurāṇa, deals with the history of Narasiṃha,
b) Nandī Upapurāṇa, where Kārtikeya has described the glory of Nandī
c) Śāmba Upapurāṇa, deals with the nature of Śāmba and
d) Āditya Upapurāṇa, deals with the nature of Āditya.[4]

## 10. Part 8.1 - Contents of the Agni Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Contents of the Agni Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8.1 - Contents of the Agni Purāṇa

According to the Matsya Purāṇa, the Purāṇa which begins with the description of Īśāna kalpa and which is narrated to Vasiṣṭha by Agni is called as the Agni Purāṇa.[1] In the Agni Purāṇa also it is defined that the Purāṇa which god Agni narrated to the sage Vasiṣṭha is known as Agni Purāṇa.[2] The Agni Purāṇa contains the great and eternal science of Brahmā, which is invisible, incomprehensible and stable and is the reason of fish and other forms. This Purāṇa was narrated to Agni by Viṣṇu, and in the ancient time it was narrated by Brahmā to the celestials.[3] The Padma Purāṇa mentions the Agni Purāṇa as a Tāmasika Purāṇa.[4] This Purāṇa is the best of all shapes that Viṣṇu had ever been assumed. According to the Agni Purāṇa itself the God Janārdana is the author and hearer of the Agni Purāṇa. Hence it is the essence of all the Vedas.[5] Variation occurs regarding the number of the verses of the Agni Purāṇa. Even this variation occurs in the Agni Purāṇa itself. In the last chapter of the Agni Purāṇa it is stated that this Purāṇa contains fifteen thousand ślokas.[6] But in an another chapter it mentions that this Purāṇa contains twelve thousand ślokas.[7] Again, the Matsya Purāṇa states that the Agni Purāṇa contains 16000 verses.[8] There are three hundred and eighty three chapters in the Agni Purāṇa.

Considering himself as Viṣṇu, Kālāgni and Rudra the god Agni converses the Agni Purāṇa which is the core of learning, best of all sciences and the source of all. Moreover, which contains accounts of creation, dissolution, various families, periods of Manus and genealogies etc.[9] The Agni Purāṇa which is excellent among the Purāṇas and which is known as Āgneya (or Agni), gives satisfaction to all the celestials. The Purāṇa is spoken by Agni and designated by the Vedas as Āgneya. It gives Bhukti (enjoyment) and Mukti (salvation) to those people, who read or hear it.

The Agni Purāṇa discusses about all branches of knowledge.[10] Knowledge according to the Agni Purāṇa is of two sorts-Parā Vidyā (the superior) and Aparā Vidyā (the inferior). The Vedas namely the Ṛgveda, the Yajurveda, the Sāmaveda and the Atharvaveda, the Vedāṅgas (the six auxiliaries of the Vedas), namely Śikṣā (phonetics), Kalpa (rules governing rituals), Vyākaraṇa (grammar), Nirukta (etymological science), Chanda (science of prosody) and Jyotiṣa (the science dealing with the movement of the luminary bodies), Abhidhāna (lexicon), Mīmāṃsā (investigation of the interpretation of the rituals of the Vedas), Dharmaśāstras(law-books), Purāṇas (18 in number), Nyāya (logical philosophical system), Vaidya (Medical Science), Gandharva (Musical Science), Dhanurveda (the science of archery) and Arthaśāstra (polity)-these all belong to Aparā Vidyā. The Parā Vidyā is that by which Brahman is realized.[11] This Purāṇa deals with the great and eternal science of Brahmā which is invisible, incomprehensible and stable.[12]

The Agni Purāṇa is sacred and admirable. It gives pleasure, satisfaction and liberation to men.[13] The superiority of Agni Purāṇa is declared in the last chapter of this Purāṇa itself. There is nothing richer in substance than the Agni Purāṇa. Nothing is a better friend than this Purāṇa. There is no greater treatise than this Purāṇa. It is the best of the scriptures, the best of the Smṛtis and the best of the philosophies. There is no superior knowledge than this Purāṇa. This Purāṇa is extremely auspicious. It is the foremost among the Purāṇas.[14] The Agni Purāṇa encloses chapter on Harivaṃśa,[15] incarnations of lord Viṣṇu as Matsya etc. (matsyāvatārādyā), Gītā, Rāmāyaṇa (gītā rāmāyaṇaṃ tviha), Mahābhārata(harivaṃśo bhārataṃ ca) etc.[16] Moreover, it deals with Āgamas of the Śaiva, Śākta and Saura, Maṇḍalas, Vāstuśāstra, various types of Mantras, Pratisarga, Brahmāṇḍa, Dvīpa, Bhuvanakoṣa etc.[17] Discussions on topics like Astrology, victory in war, Manvantaras, Prāyaścitta, Rājadharma, Dānadharma, Vratas, Sūryavaṃśa, Somavaṃśa, Dhanurveda, Āyurveda, Gāndharvaveda, Arthaśāstra, Mīmāṃsā, Nyāya etc. are also met with in this Purāṇa.[18]

## 11. Part 7 - Division of the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Division of the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 7 - Division of the Purāṇas

These Mahāpurāṇas are classified as Sāttvika, Rājasika and Tāmasika from the standpoint of the three cosmic qualities(guṇa),viz. sattva, rajas and tamas. The Purāṇas exalting Viṣṇu are called Sāttvika, those exalting Brahmā are called Rājasa, while those exalting Śiva are called Tāmasa. There are other Purāṇas which are called Saṃkīrṇas(of mixed type). In these Purāṇas, the narration centers round Sarasvatī and the manes.[1]

According to the Padma Purāṇa, the classification based on guṇa is as follows-[2]

1 Sāttvika Purāṇas—Viṣṇu, Nāradīya, Bhāgavata, Garuḍa, Padma, and Varāha.
2 Rājasika Purāṇas—Brahmāṇḍa, Brahmavaivarta, Mārkaṇḍeya, Bhaviṣya Vāmana and Brahma.
3 Tāmasika Purāṇas—Matsya, Kūrma, Liṅga,Śiva, Skanda and Agni.

The Garuḍa Purāṇa[3] also gives this classification with a slight modification. According to the Garuḍa Purāṇa, the Bhāgavata, Viṣṇu, Garuḍa, Matsya, Kūrma and Vāyu are described as Sāttvika Purāṇas.[4] Among these six Purāṇas, the Bhāgavata Purāṇa, Viṣṇu Purāṇa and Garuḍa Purāṇa are exalted as superior, as they are the object of special favor of the god Viṣṇu or Hari.[5] The Rājasa Purāṇas are the Skanda, Padma, Vāmana, Varāha, Agni and Bhaviṣya.[6] The Tāmasa Purāṇas are Brahmāṇḍa, Liṅga, Brahmavaivarta, Mārkaṇḍeya, Brahma and Āditya.[7] In the Garuḍa Purāṇa the Sāttvika Purāṇas are divided into three classes. These classes are Sāttvika uttama, Sāttvika madhyama and Sāttvika adhama. Among the Sāttvika Purāṇas, Viṣṇu, Bhāgavata and Garuḍa are Sāttvika uttama, Vāyu is Sāttvika Madhyama and Matsya and Kūrma are Sāttvika adhama.[8]

## 12. Part 8.2 - Contents of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Contents of the Bhagavata Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8.2 - Contents of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa

According to the Bhāgavata Purāṇa, Brahmā described the Bhāgavata Purāṇa to his son Nārada, which had been transmitted to him by the glorious God. Nārada, who then illustrated it to Vyāsa.[1] The glorification of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa has been stated elaborately in the Bhāgavata-Māhātmya[2] section of the Padma Purāṇa. It is stated there that in the Kali-age, the holy text of Śrīmad Bhāgavata, would bring safety of the world. There is nothing higher to this Purāṇa that would cleanse the mind. It is acquired by good men through religious merit earned in many (earlier) existences.[3] Knowing the salvation of king Parīkṣit, the lotus-born one (kamalasaṃbhavaḥ) compared the holy texts and the great Purāṇas.

Among them Śrīmad Bhāgavata came to be superior on the earth.[4] By reciting and listening to it, a man goes to Viṣṇu's position. Listening to it for a year gives great happiness, dedication to it for a month brings luxury, and listening to it for a week gives salvation at all times.[5] The Śrīmadbhāgavata Purāṇa takes pleasure in itself.

It is manifested for the happiness of Bhakti, Jñāna and Vairāgya to Brahmā, by Kṛṣṇa. The four-versed (summary) of it appears to be the entire Brahman. That account was narrated to Nārada by Brahmā. Nārada narrated it to Vyāsadeva, to take away torment. By remembering it Bādarāyaṇa (Vyāsa) instantly became depressed; and in a manner delighting the soul, composed the work of the great name.[6] According to the Padma Purāṇa, the treatise called Śrīmad Bhāgavata comprises eighteen thousand verses and twelve skandhas(sections), and is in the form of a dialogue between Parīkṣit and Śuka.[7]

The Bhāgavata Purāṇa itself too mentions that it contains eighteen thousands ślokas.[8] The Bhāgavata Purāṇa was revealed by Viṣṇu to god Brahmā at first.[9] It contains discussion on the value of detachment at the beginning, in the middle and at the end. Moreover, it contains several episodes exalting the sportive activities of Lord Hari.[10] The Bhāgavata Purāṇa is said to be the essence of all the Upaniṣads which is distinguished by uniqueness between the supreme Brahman and the Paramātman. Its only purpose is to secure Mokṣa.[11] One, who drinks the sweet nectar of the teachings of this Purāṇa, does not find pleasure in any other thing.[12] The Bhāgavata Purāṇa occupies the foremost position among all the other Purāṇas.The position is compared with that of the holiest river Gaṅgā among the other rivers, Lord Viṣṇu among the gods or god Śiva among the devotees of Viṣṇu.[13] Among the Purāṇas there is no Purāṇa which can be equal to the Bhāgavata Purāṇa.[14] It is a Purāṇa without any fault. It is dear to the devotees of Śrī Viṣṇu.[15] He who, on the full moon day of Bhādrapada, offers the Bhāgavata Purāṇa as a gift, attains to the highest abode.[16]

In the Bhāgavata Purāṇa the god has been glorified at every step, who abides in the entire universe.[17] Lord Hari who is the destroyer of all sins, who is Nārāyaṇa, the regulator of sense-organs and who is the defender of his devotees, has been glorified here, in this Purāṇa.[18] It has described the Supreme Brahma, who is the cause of creation. The topic of spiritual knowledge has also been discussed.[19] In its twelfth Skandha, the Bhāgavata Purāṇa itself mentions the merits of studying it.

According to statement mentioned therein, a Brāhmaṇa acquires spiritual knowledge, a Kṣatriya conquers sovereignty over the whole world, a Vaiśya becomes the lord of possessions and a Śūdra is freed of sins, on studying this Purāṇa.[20] He who narrates this Purāṇa every day or for a time of three hours or for a minute and he who listens this Purāṇa with devotion, purifies his inner soul.[21] Moreover, it added that one, who hears it on the twelfth or eleventh day of every fortnight of the lunar month, will be blessed with long life. One who reads it with dedication frees himself of all sins.[22] Reading this Purāṇa at the holy places like Puṣkara, Mathurā and Dvārakā, is said to be freed from the fear of Saṃsāra.[23] One who, with a pure and controlled mind, learns this Purāṇa, attains the supreme abode i.e. Mokṣa.[24]

In this regard Professor Wilson’s remark is worthy to mention. He thus views: The Bhāgavata is a work of great celebrity in India and exercises a more direct and powerful influence upon the opinions and feelings of the people than perhaps any other of the Purāṇas. It is placed in the fifth position in all the lists but the Padma-Purāṇa ranks it as the eighteenth, as the extracted substance of all the rest. According to the usual specification, it consists of eighteen thousand ślokas, distributed amongst three hundred and thirty-two chapters divided into twelve Skandhas or books.[25]

The Bhāgavata Purāṇa[26] has ten topics in it. They are as follows—

1. Sarga (subtle creation)—It consists of the creation of the Bhūtas, Tanmātras, Indriyas, Ahaṃkāra and Mahat, and the appearance of Virāṭ Puruṣa.[27]
2 Visarga (gross creation)—the individual creation by Brahmā is called Visarga.[28]
3 Sthāna [law and order (ensured by God)]—it is the triumph of the lord in the maintenance of (the divine) law and order(in everything).[29]
4 Poṣaṇa (protection-welfare of all)—the grace of God is known as Poṣaṇa.[30]
5 Manvantara (the periods of Manu)—the duties of the rulers of Manvantaras.[31]
6 Ūti (material lust from Karmas)—the desire for karma(action) is called Ūti.[32]
7 Īśānukathā (accounts of God’s deeds)—the stories of the various incarnations and of the followers of Hari are known as Īśānukathā.[33]
8 Nirodha (physical annihilation)—the sleep of Hari and all individual souls in Pralaya.[34]
9 Mukti (liberation)—the continued perception of the identity of self and of Brahman.[35]
10 Āśraya (the last resort of the universe, the ultimate reality)—The Final Resort, Para-Brahman or Paramātman from creation or dissolution both proceed.[36]

The Bhāgavata Purāṇa contains twelve Skandhas. The first Skandha presents a discourse between Vyāsa and Nārada,[37] as well as accounts of Nārada.[38] The second Skandha describes what men are to do and what they are forbidden to do,[39] about the creation of the Universe-the Bhāgavata Purāṇa as related by Brahmā to Nārada,[40] description of the Virāṭ Puruṣa,[41] preservation by Līlā-Avatāras[42].The third Skandha describes about the creation of Bhāgavata as related by Maitreya to Vidura,[43] the ten-fold creation by Brahmā,[44] divisions of Kāla,[45] the descriptions of the various creations of Brahmā,[46] the first or the Svāyambhuva–Manvantara,[47] Deva and Deva-yoni creation.[48] Kapila, the founder of Sāṃkhya school is mentioned in the third Skandha as an incarnation of Viṣṇu, who instructs his mother Devāhuti about the yoga philosophy of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa.[49] The Fourth Skandha describes about the genealogy of Manu and the Ṛṣis,[50] the story of Dhruva,[51] the story of Pṛthu, etc.[52] The fifth and the sixth Skandha describes about the Bhuvanakoṣa, Earth-chain,[53] story of Ajāmila,[54] the progeny of Dakṣa,[55] the story of Vṛtra,[56] etc. The seventh Skandha includes the account of Hiraṇyakaśipu and Prahlāda,[57] description of Varṇa and Āśrama,[58] etc. The eight Skandha gives description about the second to fourteenth Manvantaras. The ninth Skandha narrates about the Vaivasvata-Manvantara,[59] the dynasty of Ikṣvāku,[60] the Lunar dynasty,[61] etc. The tenth Skandha includes the description of the life of Kṛṣṇa. The love-scenes with the Gopīs have been described elaborately. Referring to the tenth Skandha Winternitz says that the tenth book is the most popular and most widely read by all. This book is translated into almost all popular Indian languages of India and is a favourite book of all classes of the Indian folk.[62] The eleventh Skandha describes about the destruction of the Yādavas and the death of Kṛṣṇa, and the twelfth Skandha encloses the usual predictions on the Kaliyuga and the destruction of the world.

## 13. Part 8.3 - Contents of the Matsya Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Contents of the Matsya Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8.3 - Contents of the Matsya Purāṇa

The Matsya Purāṇa is one of the 18 Mahā-Purāṇas consisting of two hundred and ninety one chapters as printed in the Ānanda Āśrama Edition, Poona.[1] The Matsya Purāṇa is a voluminous work which begins with the story of the great flood.Here Viṣṇu, in the form of a fish(Matsya) saves Manu alone. The conversation which took place between Manu and Viṣṇu, incarnated as a fish, forms the substance of the Purāṇa.[2] It is a conglomeration of chapters taken from various sources, especially the Vāyu Purāṇa and Viṣṇu Dharmottara Purāṇa.[3]

The Purāṇa, in which the lord speaks the glory of Narasiṃha to Vaivasvata Manu at the beginning of the Kalpa with a view to spread the Śrutis and in which the lord has described about the seven kalpas, is known as the Matsya Purāṇa. The Matsya Purāṇa contains fourteen thousand ślokas.[4] But according to the Agni Purāṇa the Matsya Purāṇa contains thirteen thousand verses.[5] All the characteristics of the Purāṇa occur in the Matsya Purāṇa. The sarga section of this Purāṇa describes about production of the grass, trees, creepers, shrubs, Yakṣas, Rākṣasas,[6] Apsaras, kinnaras and Gandharvas[7] etc. The pratisarga section describes about the various manvantaras, the stories of all the Manus and the rule of each one of them.[8] The Vaṃśa section is found to be dealt with the descriptions of the family and the sect of Sūrya, Soma and Pitṛs.[9] The features of the age of manvantaras has been described in the manvantara section.[10] The Vaṃśānucarita part of the Matsya Purāṇa includes descriptions about the ruling of different dynasties of Ikṣvākus and Bṛhadrathas,[11] Pradyuta, Śiśunāka, the Nanda, the Maurya, the Śuṅgas and the Kanvāyana dynasties.[12] The Matsya Purāṇa also gives the description of Bhāratavarṣa, Kiṃpuruṣa and Harivarṣa.[13] Regarding the name Bhāratavarṣa it is stated that the place in which one can achieves the svarga (heaven), mokṣa(emancipation) and the position between the two, is known as Bhāratavarṣa.[14] This Purāṇa again describes the Earth and its boundary with the seven Dvīpas and their extent and other measurements.[15] It also describes various incarnations of Viṣṇu such as Nṛsiṃha,[16] Vāmana,[17] Varāha,[18] etc. Description of Rājadharma and its inflicting punishments[19], the reigns of the Andhras, Yavanas and the Mlecchas are also described in this Purāṇa.[20]

There are descriptions about glorification of the sacred places of Allahabad (Prayāga Māhātmya),[21] Benares (Vārānasī and Avimukta-Māhātmya),[22] of the river Narmadā,[23] the duties of a king,[24] the ceremonies at the building of a house,[25] the excretion and dedication of statues of deities, temples and palaces,[26] the sixteen kinds of donations,[27] etc.

As far as religious content is concerned, the Matsya Purāṇa might be called Śaivite with just as much reason as it is classed as Viṣṇuite. Religious festivals of the Vaiṣṇavas are described side by side with those of the Śaivas, and both Viṣṇu and Śiva-legends are related.[28]

## 14. Part 8.4 - Contents of the Vāyu Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Contents of the Vayu Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8.4 - Contents of the Vāyu Purāṇa

The Vāyu Purāṇa obtains its name from Vāyu who is its promulgator. M.Winternitz in his A History of Indian Literature says that this Purāṇa appears in some lists, under the name of Śaiva or Śiva Purāṇa, a title which is given to the work because it is dedicated to the worship of god Śiva. As proclaimed by the Wind-god, Vāyu Purāṇa is quoted in the Mahābhārata as well as by the Harivaṃśa. It has been mentioned that the poet Bāṇa (about 625 A.D.) had read the Vāyu Purāṇa and that in this Purāṇa, the rule of the Guptas is described as it was in the 4th century A.D., making the work not later than 5th century A.D.[1]

The Vāyu Purāṇa itself declares that it was illustrated by Vāyu (Mātariśvā).[2] It was recited to the Brāhmaṇas by Vāyu.[3] It was expanded by Vāyu himself, who was employed in the beneficence of the people. He described it to the sages in the Naimiṣa forest.[4] In another verse, the Vāyu Purāṇa states that this Purāṇa was spoken by Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana, who was the expounder of Brahman.[5] Again it is stated that previously this Purāṇa had been stated by Brahmā.[6] This sacred text was presented by Brahmā to Mātariśvā(Vāyu). From him it was acquired by Uśanas, from Uśanas it was acquired by Bṛhaspati, Bṛhaspati then spoke it to Savitṛ. Savitṛ passed it to Mṛtyu(Yama), Mṛtyu passed it to Indra, Indra passed it to Vasiṣṭha and Vasiṣṭha passed it to Sārasvata. Sārasvata passed it to Tridhāmā, Tridhāmā passed it to Śaradvata, Śaradvata passed it to Triviṣṭa and Triviṣṭa passed it to Antarikṣa.

Antarikṣa passed it to Varṣiṇ, Varṣiṇ passed it to Trayyāruṇa, Trayyāruṇa passed it to Dhanañjaya and he gave it to Kṛtañjaya. From Kṛtañjaya it was received by Tṛṇañjaya, he passed it to Bharadvāja, Bharadvāja passed it on to Gautama and Gautama gave it to Niryantara. Niryantara handed it over to Vājaśravas, Vājaśravas passed it on to Somaśuṣma, who gave it to Tṛṇabindu. Tṛṇabindu gave it to Dakṣa, Dakṣa passed it to Śakti, from Śakti it was heard by Parāśara, who was even in embryo then. From Parāśara it was received by Jātukarṇa, he passed it to Dvaipāyana and from Dvaipāyana, it was expanded to Śāṃśapāyana.[7]

The Vāyu Purāṇa elaborates the spirits of manes (Pitṛs) and their cults (Śrāddhas).[8] It’s one chapter is devoted to the art of song.[9] Although it is a Śaiva Purāṇa, but we find two chapters in this Purāṇa, even on Viṣṇu too.[10] Moreover, in the Gayā Māhātmya section, a great tribute is paid to Viṣṇu.[11]

The whole Vāyu Purāṇa is divided into two parts(Kāṇḍa).The second Kāṇḍa begins with the 62nd chapter.[12] There are one hundred and twelve chapters in this Purāṇa. It has twenty-three thousand Ślokas.[13]

This Purāṇa is divided into four Pādas—

Prakriyā Pāda,

Ānuṣaṅga Pāda,

Upodghāta Pāda, and

Upasaṃhāra Pāda.[14]

Leaving out the two opening chapters, the first section (Prakriyā Pāda) occupies only four chapters, with the general title of sṛṣṭiprakaraṇaṃ or the creation of world.[15]

The second section (Ānusaṅga Pāda) comprises 58 chapters of the book, beginning with the 7th chapter and ending with the 64th chapter.[16] It is the longest among the four sections. Creation of gods and men, cycles of Manus, practice of Yoga, descriptions of thirty-three Kalpas of the duration of the world(the last one of these kalpas i.e., Śveta Kalpa, in which we are now said to have), incarnations of Mahādeva, and the curse of Dakṣa, descendants of Ṛṣis, of Agni and other Devas, of Svāyambhuva Manu, of Prajāpati, of Pṛthu, the descriptions of Jambudvīpa, the four yugas, the sacred tīrthas, the continents of Ketumāla, Bhāratavarṣa, Kiṃpuruṣa and Plakṣa are also met with in this section.

The third section (Upodghāta Pāda) contains 34 chapters (65-99).[17] This section gives the description of the races of Prajāpati, Kāśyapa, Varuṇa, Vaivasvata Manu, Viṣṇu, and the Gandharvas. Moreover, there are accounts of Śrāddhas to which more than ten chapters are devoted. Descendants of Soma and Yadu race and the dynasties of kali age are also met with there.

The last section (Upasaṃhāra Pāda) consists of chapters 100 to the end.[18] Periods of seven Manus[19] are described here. Moreover, descriptions of periodical destructions and regenerations[20] have also been elaborated. The concluding chapters on the greatness of Gayā seem to be out of place and indicate a definite interpolation.[21] The Vāyu Purāṇa states that, a Brāhmaṇa, who may understand the four Vedas along with the six Vedāṅgas and Upaniṣads, but if he does not understand this Purāṇa then he may be called as an inexpert.[22]

## 15. Part 10 - Literature Review


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Literature Review’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Part 10 - Literature Review

A review of both the original and modern works, which helped me to get the work in this present form, has been provided below:

The Purāṇas Published by Venkateswar Steam Press, Mumbai, Nag Publishers, New Delhi, Chaukhamba Sanskrit Pratisthan, New Delhi and Navabharat Publishers, Kolkata have been used for the present study. Moreover, Agnimahāpurāṇam, edited by K.L. Joshi and translated by M.N. Dutta and published by Parimal Publications, Bhāgavata Mahāpurāṇam, edited by Dr. Puspendra Kumar, translated by Manmath Nath Dutt and published by Eastern Book Linkers, Vāyu Mahāpurāṇam, translated by Sudarshan Kumar Sarmah and published by Parimal Publications, Viṣṇu-Purāṇam, edited by K.L. Joshi, translated by H.H. Wilson and published by Parimal Publications, Garuḍa Mahāpurāṇam, edited by Pushpendra Kumar and published by Eastern Book Linkers, Matsya Mahāpurāṇam, edited and translated by K.L. Joshi and published by Parimal Publications have also been used. For understanding the meaning of verses of the Purāṇas, English Translation of the Purāṇas edited by J.L.Shastri, G.P. Bhatt and N.Gangadharan, published under the Ancient Indian Tradition and Mythology Series by Motilal Banarsidass needs special mention. Mention must be made of the treatise Nirukta of Yāska, which gives the etymologies of the Vedic words.

For the Introduction of the Purāṇas The Agni Purāṇa-A Study by S.D. Gyani, Studies on the Purāṇic Records of Hindu Rites and Customs by R.C. Hazra, Some Aspects of the Vāyu Purāṇa by V.R.R. Dikshitar, Ancient Indian Historical Tradition by F.E. Pargiter, History of Indian Literature, volume 1 and 3 by M. Winternitz, A Study of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa or Esoteric Hinduism by Purnendu Narayana Sinha, Matsya Purāṇa -A Study by V.S. Agarwal and Purāṇavimarśa by Baladeva Upadhyaya were very much useful. The following books have been utilized as source materials for the study of Agriculture and Animal Husbandry-History of Agriculture in India(up to c. 1200 AD),published under the History of Science, Philosophy and Culture in Indian Civilization series, volume 5, part 1, edited by Lallanji Gopal & V.C. Srivastava, Saṃskṛtvāṅmaye kṛṣivijñānam by Dr. Navalata, The Vedic Age by R.C. Majumder, Agriculture and Animal Husbandry in the Vedas by N.M. Kansara, Encyclopaedia of Purāṇic Beliefs and Practices in five Volumes by S.A. Dange, Prācīn Bhāratīya Kṛṣi Vidyā written on Hindi by Bal Krishna Tiwari and The Purāṇic Index in three volumes by V.R.R. Dikshitar.

Agricultural Practices in Ancient India: A Preliminary Study by S.P. Raychaudhuri, Agriculture in Ancient India-A Report by S.P. Raychaudhuri & Mira Roy, Agriculture & Allied Arts in Vedic India by A.K. Yegna Narayan Aiyer and A Critical Study of the Bhāgavata-Purāṇa by T.S. Rukmani has also dealt with. P.V. Kane’s History of Dharmaśāstra, volume 2, part 2 is also noteworthy. Mention must be made of Amarakoṣa of Amarasiṃha edited by Visvanath Jha, which helped to know the meaning of the words.

The Bhāgavata Purāṇa: Mytho-Social Study by S.S. Dange, Purāṇic Encyclopaedia by Vettam Mani, Vedic Mythological Tracts by R.N. Dandekar, Ṛgvedic Culture by Abinas Chandra Das and Hindu Mythology (Vedic and Purāṇic) by W.J. Wilkins deserve special mention for the study of the myths related to agriculture. The post-Purāṇic works which deal with the various aspects of agriculture and animal husbandry are the Kṛṣi Pārāśara of Parāśara, edited by Ramachandra Pandeya, Upavana-Vinoda of Śārṅgadhara edited by G.P. Majumder, Vṛkṣāyurveda of Surapāla edited by Gyanendra Pandey, Viśvavallabha of Chakrapāṇi Miśra edited by Nalini Sadhale, Kāśyapīyakṛṣisūkti of Kāśyapa edited by S.M. Ayachit, Bṛhatsaṃhitā of Varāhamihira edited by V.S. Sastri & M.R. Bhat, Śukranītisāra of Śukra edited by Sri Jivananda Vidyasagar Bhattacharyya and Arthaśāstra of Kauṭilya edited by R.Shamasastry The Cultural Heritage of India, volume 2 & 6, edited by S.Radhakrishnan has also contributed a lot to the field of agriculture and animal husbandry. For having the concept of agriculture and animal husbandry in the 20-21st Century, the books A History of Agriculture in India in four volumes by M.S. Randhawa, A Textbook on Ancient History of Indian Agriculture by R.C. Saxena, S.L. Choudhary, & Y.L. Nene and published by Asian Agri History Foundation, Agriculture and Animal Husbandry in India by M.S. Randhawa, Principles of Crop Husbandry by Y.K. Yegna Narayan Aiyer, A Handbook of Animal Husbandry and Dairying by V.T. Subbiah Mudaliar, Handbook of Animal Husbandry edited by R.R. Lokeshwar, Handbook of Indian Agriculture by Nitya Gopal Mukerji, Whither Agriculture in India by Baljit Singh, Lessons on Indian Agriculture by D. Clouston and Agriculture in Assam by A.C. Thakur are worth mentioning.

Hence, it is seen that till now there are various works on agriculture and animal husbandry contributed by a number of authors. But for lack of space all the treatises could not be traced out here.

## 16. Part 9 - Justification of Studying the Five Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Justification of Studying the Five Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Part 9 - Justification of Studying the Five Purāṇas

The title of the thesis Agriculture and Animal Husbandry in the Purāṇas-A Study itself indicates that the study should have included the whole eighteen Purāṇas. But among the Eighteen Mahāpurāṇas the study of only five Purāṇas is included in this dissertation. They are the Agni Purāṇa, the Bhāgavata Purāṇa, the Matsya Purāṇa, the Vāyu Purāṇa and the Viṣṇu Purāṇa. The reason of taking these aforesaid five Purāṇas is mainly lack of time and space. Due to the limitation of time during my research period, the study of the aforesaid five Purāṇas is considered.

Because, it would not have been possible for me to complete the study of the whole eighteen Purāṇas in just five years. The contents of the whole eighteen Purāṇas are so vast, so it would have taken more than five years to have a thorough look into the whole eighteen Purāṇas. Furthermore, these five Purāṇas were thought to be containing more data on agriculture and animal husbandry than in the remaining thirteen Purāṇas, comparatively. In this matter my Preceptors guided me. So following their suggestions I selected to do research covering these five Purāṇas.

Moreover, the limitation of the length of the thesis also compelled me to take these five Purāṇas mainly. The length of the thesis should be 300 pages according to the Ph.D. guidelines. A candidate must not cross this limit. I was also bound to keep this rule in view.

## 17. Part 8.5 - Contents of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Contents of the Vishnu Purana’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8.5 - Contents of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa

The Viṣṇu Purāṇa is a sectarian work belonging to the Pañcatantras, and purely Vaiṣṇava from beginning to end.[1] In this work Viṣṇu is praised and glorified as the supreme being, as the one and only god with whom also Brahmā and Śiva are identical.[2] The Viṣṇu Purāṇa is an early work composed most probably in the last quarter of the third or the first quarter of the fourth century A.D.[3] Pargiter in his Ancient Indian Historical Tradition, too opines that it is a late Purāṇa composed as a single whole upon a consistent plan, and not a collection of materials of various times, as we find in the Vāyu, Brahma and Matsya. From its account of Buddhism, and Jainism it appears to have been composed after Brāhmaṇism had recovered its supremacy, so that it cannot be earlier than about the 5th century A.D., and it is Brāhmaṇical.[4] According to the Agni Purāṇa, the Viṣṇu Purāṇa was narrated by sage Parāśara. It deals with the events which were occurred in the cycle of time known as the Varāha-kalpa. This Purāṇa contains twenty three thousand verses.[5] In the last chapter of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa it is declared that this Purāṇa is the best of all preservatives for one who is frightened of worldly reality and this is the remover of all deficiencies.[6]

The origin of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa is cited in the last chapter of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa itself. According to it, this Purāṇa was originally composed by the sage Nārāyaṇa. Then it was spoken by Brahmā to Ṛbhu. Ṛbhu imparted it to Priyavrata. Priyavrata then communicated it to Bhāguri.[7] Bhāguri gave it to Tambamitra, and Tambamitra to Dadhīci. Dadhīci recited it to Sārasvata, Sārasvata gave it to Bhṛgu.[8] Bhṛgu imparted it to Purukutsa, who then communicated it to Narmadā. Narmadā imparted it to the Nāga king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and to Apūraṇa of the same race,[9] by whom it was then communicated to their monarch Vāsuki. Vāsuki imparted it to Vatsa, and Vatsa to Aśvatara.[10] Aśvatara repeated it to Kambala and Elāpatra After that the sage Vedaśirā going down to Pātāla, received the whole Purāṇa from the Nāgas.[11] Vedaśirā gave it to Pramati, and Pramati communicated it to intelligent Jātūkarṇa.[12] Jātūkarṇa taught it to some other holy persons, and through Vasiṣṭha’s consents it came to Parāśara and Parāśara finally imparted it to Maitreya.[13] It is divided into six books called aṃśas.

Again, each of the parts consists of a number of chapters.

(1) The first book is occupied chiefly with the details of creation, primary (sarga) and secondary (pratisarga); the first i.e., sarga explains how the universe proceeds from prakṛti or eternal crude matter; the second i.e., pratisarga explains in what manner the forms of things are developed from the elementary substances previously evolved, or how they reappear after their temporary destruction.[14] This book begins with a dialogue between Parāśara, the grandson of Vasiṣṭha and his pupil Maitreya. Maitreya inquires Parāśara, the origin and nature of the universe.

This book deals with details of creation, recreation[15], measure of time[16], origin of the four castes[17],origin of Rudra[18], legend of the churning of ocean[19], legend of Dhruva[20], story of Vena and Pṛthu[21], legend of Prahlāda[22], etc.

(2) The second book opens with a continuation of the kings of the first Manvantara; amongst whom, Bharata is said to have given a name to India, called, after him, Bhārata-varṣa.[23] It gives a fantastic description of the world at first[24]. The seven continents and the seven oceans are described, in the midst of which Jambudvīpa is found with the golden mountain of Meru-the abode of the gods. In Jambudvīpa lies Bhāratavarṣa i.e. India whose countries, mountains and rivers are enumerated. In connection with the name Bhāratavarṣa, then[25] a legend is told of the ancient king Bharata.

(3) The third book deals firstly with the account of the several Manus and Manvantaras, then, names of the twenty-eight Vyāsas who lived in different ages, division of the Vedas[26],duties of religious student, householder, hermit[27], funeral sacrifices,[28] etc.

(4) The fourth book contains all that the Hindus have of their ancient history. It is a tolerably comprehensive list of dynasties and individuals: it is a barren record of events.[29] It deals with dynasties of the kings, birth of Ikṣvāku,[30] dynasty of the Moon,[31] dynasties of the kings of the Kali age,[32] etc.

(5) The fifth book of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa is exclusively occupied with the life of Kṛṣṇa.[33] It is the longest book in this Purāṇa which gives us the account of marriage of Vāsudeva and Devakī, Yogamāyā who enters into the womb of Yaśodā and Hari who enters into the womb of Devakī,[34] holding of Govardhana Mountain by Kṛṣṇa,[35] Kṛṣṇa’s marriage with Rukmiṇī,[36] destruction of the Yadu race[37] etc.

The last book contains an account of the dissolution of the world, in both its major and minor cataclysms; and, in the particulars of the end of all things by fire and water, as well as in the principle of their perpetual renovation, presents a faithful exhibition of opinions that were general in the ancient world.[38] The sixth book is the shortest among all the books. It describes the nature of the Kali age,[39] nature of elemental dissolution (prakṛta pralaya),[40] description of Brahma yoga, and the greatness of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa.[41]

## 18. Part 2 - The Meaning of the Word Kṛṣi (Agriculture)


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Meaning of the Word Krishi (Agriculture)’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 2 - The Meaning of the Word Kṛṣi (Agriculture)

The word kṛṣi is used to denote agriculture, which literally means the act of ploughing. It is derived from the root kṛṣ which means to plough, till, cultivate with the suffix ik.[1] The commentator Bhānuji Dīkṣit explains the word as supported by the vārttika ‘ikkṛṣyādibhyaḥ’ i.e. √kṛṣ means vilekhana meaning digging.

Further Amarakoṣa mentions kṛṣi as one of the Vṛttis i.e. livelihood of Aryans such as—

kṛṣiḥ paśupālyaṃ vāṇijyaṃ ceti vṛttayaḥ.[2]

kṛṣiḥ paśupālyaṃ vāṇijyaṃ ceti vṛttayaḥ.[2]

In the Śabdakalpadruma, the word kṛṣi is explained as the occupation of the Vaiśyas—

vaiśyavṛttiviśeṣaḥ.[3]

vaiśyavṛttiviśeṣaḥ.[3]

M. Monier Williams in his A Sanskrit-English Dictionary defines kṛṣi as ploughing, cultivation of the soil, agriculture (one of the vṛttis of Vaiśyas).[4] The Nighaṇṭu records the word kṛṣṭayaḥ (ploughing, cultivating the soil) among the synonyms of the term manuṣya (man).[5]

The Nirukta adds that kṣetraṃ (abode) is derived from the root kṣi, meaning to dwell; hence kṣetram is the dwelling-place and kṣetrasyapati is its protector or supporter[6]—

kṣetrasya patiḥ/ kṣetraṃ kṣiyaternivāsakarmaṇaḥ/tasya pātā vā pālayitā vā//.[7]

kṣetrasya patiḥ/ kṣetraṃ kṣiyaternivāsakarmaṇaḥ/tasya pātā vā pālayitā vā//.[7]

Here mention can be made of the word kṣetrapati as it has been revealed by the seer Vāmadeva in the Ṛgveda.[8] Here in this hymn the Vedic God Rudra has been worshipped as the lord of kṣetra i.e. a field or a fertile soil.[9] There are many ideas related to agriculture in this hymn.A study of this hymn shows that the Vedic seer Vāmadeva was quite conversant with ancient Indian advanced agriculture.The term kṛṣi is a feminine word.It also means karṣaṇam. Amarakoṣa has given the synonyms of kṛṣīvala as such, kṣetrājīvaḥ karṣakaśca kṛṣakaśca etc. Here kṛṣīvalaḥ means farmer.In this regard Amarasiṃha refers to kṣetrājīva i.e. a farmer who lives by land i.e. the act of ploughing.[10]

## 20. Part 1 - Introduction and history of ancient Indian Agriculture


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Introduction and history of ancient Indian Agriculture’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 1 - Introduction and history of ancient Indian Agriculture

Agriculture is one of the most essential aspects of human life. Agriculture and allied activities have been providing subsistence for most of the population, since the ancient period. Agriculture came to be practised when man gave up his nomadic habits and settled in favourable climate and topography.Initially depending on wild roots, fruits and seeds for his sustenance, man eventually adopted the practice of tilling the land to grow crops.[1] Archeologists and historians opine that in the Indian subcontinent, agricultural activities began around 7000 BC in the Neolithic period.The only remains of agricultural settlements of that period are found at Mehrgarh, i.e. presently situated in Baluchistan of Pakistan. But if the climatic condition, ecological situation, implements of hunting etc., mentioned in the Zend Avesta (the sacred book of Zoroastrianism) of the Middle East (the transcontinental region centered on Western Asia, Turkey and Egypt) and in the Ṛgveda of India are compared, it becomes clear that the evolution of agricultural practices in India simultaneously took place with the Middle East about 10,000 BC.[2] Both these holy treatises are of the same period and deal with the same thought regarding agricultural practices. Although it has not been ascertained when the early inhabitants of India took the farming as their chief occupation, the practice of agriculture has been traced back to the Indus Valley Civilization.Therefore, for at least the last 4500 years the Indian society has been primarily an agricultural one.[3]

Although Indus Valley Civilization, according to available facts, is supposed to be an urban civilization, yet agriculture was the mainstay of life and provided sound base to their economy. The archaeological remains of large granaries of Harappa and Mahenjo-daro prove it that agricultural activities systematically developed fully.[4] The discovery, of some stone implements ‘much too heavy to have served as weapons’ suggests that these were ploughshares ‘quite efficient in the stoneless alluvial soil of the Indus plains’.[5] Two incomplete curved blades of copper recovered from Mahenjo-daro were probably used as sickles for harvesting purposes.[6]

Harappan people cultivated several cereals, pulses, oilseeds, fibre crops, fruits and vegetables.They knew crops of different seasons like kharif and rabi.Among cereals they cultivated wheat, barley, rice, jowar, bajra(pearl millet), ragi(finger millet), foxtail(Italian millet);among pulses lentil, chick pea(Bengal gram), field pea, green gram, black gram; linseed, mustard, sesame and among fibre crops they cultivated cotton.[7] Information of animal husbandry is also found. The figure of a bull on a Mahenjo-daro seal is interpreted as indicating that great importance was attached to cattle breeding in those days.The skeletal remains of the humped bull, buffalo, sheep, elephant, and camel unearthed in the Indus valley area show that these animals were domesticated.[8]

Agriculturists in ancient India were quite aware of the nature of soil and its relations to the production of a particular crop of economic importance. The Aryans were an active pastoral people and agriculture was the backbone of economy at that time.[9] According to the historians, the Aryan tribes entered India from Central Asia in the 2nd millennium BC.[10] The Vedas are the main source of information regarding agricultural activities practiced by the Aryans. The Vedas attach great importance to agriculture. Among the ancient Vedic treatises mainly the Ṛgveda records various aspects of agriculture. In the Ṛgveda, the gambler is advised to stop gambling and engage himself in agriculture to improve his condition.[11] The Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa has mentioned the four principal operations of agriculture. They are ploughing, sowing, reaping and threshing.[12] It also refers to the practice of yoking six, twelve or twenty-four bullocks.[13] The epic Rāmāyaṇa clearly states that people, who are engaged in agriculture and cattle rearing, can live happily and can prosper in life.[14] The Purāṇas too provide many additional aspects regarding agriculture.These are discussed in details in this chapter.
