Footnotes.

Part 21 - Treatment and Care of Horses

About the health care of horses the Garuḍa Purāṇa advises as follows-the rite of protection of horses should be done by worshipping the God Revanta and by feeding Brāhmaṇas. To prevent from diseases, the essence of the sarala tree, margosa (nimba) leaves, guggula, sarṣapa, ghṛta, tila, vacā, and hiṅgu should be tied to the neck of the horse.[1] The Garuḍa Purāṇa recommends the following points that are to be observed while taking care of horses—

1) For curing the wounds a ball prepared from the garlic, rock salt, butter milk, gruel, oil, malt, margosa leaves should be used.[2]

2) For curing germs in the stomach, compound of paṭola, nimba-patra, vacā, citraka, pippalī, triphalā and śṛṅgavera should be used.[3]

3) Decoction of nimba leaves, paṭola, triphalā and khadira is prescribed to be given for three days in the disease called haya-kuṣṭha(diseases that affects the skin).[4]

4) In any kind of hurt or injury, the horse may be given guggula, to drink and should be made to drink pāyasa.Then he would be hale in loss of time.[5]

5) In case of any diseases one palaṃ weight of dūrvā grass should be given to a horse in the first day. The dose should be increased by measure of one karṣa each day, till it reaches five palaṃ. Eighty palaṃ is the highest dose, sixty is the middle and fourty palaṃ is considered the lowest.[6]

6) If he suffers from any eating trouble, he should be given milk and boiled rice as food. In pitta the same with flesh juice, honey, soup of green gram and ghee is to be given. For cough green gram and kullattha should be administered[7]

7) In diseases that affect the skin, in lameness and in swellings, triphalā boiled on mild fire along with cow’s urine is to be administered.[8]

8) In vāta and pitta together or in vraṇa(ulcer) ghee mixed with cow milk should be given; and to faten the unsteady horses food with flesh should be given.[9]

9) As a general tonic five palas of guḍucī, well powdered, should be given mixed with ghee in the morning, in the autumn and in the hot season; also śatāvarī and aśvagandhā should be prepared with guḍucī. Four palas and three palas should be given respectively to the moderate horse and the horse of a lesser quality.[10]

In the Agni Purāṇa, Śālihotra talks about the warning marks that are usually found in the body of a horse and the diseases it generally suffers from.[11] The diseases of horses and their remedies according to the Agni Purāṇa are as follows:

Bloody urine(raktameha): Goat’s milk mixed with sugar and cooled after being boiled with mañjīṣṭhā, madhuka, drākṣā, bṛhatī, raktacandana, roots and seeds of trapuṣa, śṛṅgāṭaka and kaserukā, should be given to the horse suffering from bloody urine.[12]

Dysentery(atīsāra): When it suffers from dysentery (atīsāra), a small piece of food consist of vṛṣa, nimba, bṛhatī, guḍūcī, siṃhī and gandhakarī should be given. A fomentation of the head with hiṅgu, roots of puṣkara, nāgara, amlavetasa, pippalī with saindhava salt and warm water would also be beneficial. Moreover, a decoction of nāgara, ativiṣa, ananta, musta and vilva leaves may also be a good remedy.[13]

Extreme tiredness(śrama): A drink composed of goat’s milk, priyaṅgu, sārivā and plentiful sugar would take away the extreme tiredness of a horse.[14]

Swelling of limbs(śotha): In case of swelling of limbs, composition of lodhra and karañja roots, mātuluṅga, agni, nāgara, kuṣṭha, hiṅgu, vacā, rāsnā would be favourable.[15]

Swelling of throat (manyāhanunigālasthaśirāśotha): In all cases of lock-jaw and in case of swelling that affects the throat, the affected part should be anointed with the medicated kaṭu taila.[16]

Cough (kāsa): When it suffers from a sudden attack of cough, food composed of dāḍima, triphalā, vyoṣa and guḍa would be beneficial. A decoction of priyaṅgu, lodhra, juice of vṛṣa with honey would also be favourable. A decoction of kṣīra and pañcakola may also be administered.[17]

Infection of throat (galagrahagadaḥ śothaḥ): When it suffers from diseases which affect the throat, then decoction composed of the flowers of pratyaka, vahni, saindhava salt, juice of saurabha, kṛṣṇā and hiṅgu would be beneficial.[18]

Paralysis of the tongue(jihvāstambha): In paralysis of the tongue, paste composed of niśa, jyotiṣmatī, pāṭhā, kṛṣṇā,kuṣṭha and vacā with madhu should be administered.[19]

Pain in abdomen(basti): To relieve a horse of any abdominal pain, he should be given the insertion of oil from a tub or the veins of his abdomen should be cut.[20]

Ulcer (vraṇa): In case of ulcer, clarified butter prepared with sesamum, yaṣṭi, rajanī, nimba leaves or paste composed of the aforesaid drugs mixed with honey would be a good remedy.[21]

Wound (vraṇa): For washing a wound and for antiseptic purposes, decoction composed of the barks of aśvattha, udumbara, plakṣa, madhūka, vilva and vaṭa boiled with abundant water should be used as a remedy.[22]

Bleeding (raktasrāva) and infection of eyes (netraroga): In cases of bleeding or in diseases which affect the end of the eyes of a horse,the affected area can be washed with a decoction composed of udumbara, aśvattha, dhātrī, tikta and priyaṅgu.[23]

Itching of skin (kaṇḍu): When it suffers from itching of the skin, a paste composed of gośakṛn, mañjikā, kuṣṭha, rajanī, tila, sarṣapa, with the urine of a cow should be administered.[24]

Healing snuffing(mrakṣaṇe bastinasye ca): For the purpose of healing snuffing, decoction prepared from śatāhvā, nāgara, rāsnā, mañjiṣṭhā, kuṣṭha, saindhava salt, devadāru, vacā, two sorts of rajanī, raktacandana, juice of guḍucī should be used.[25]

Excess of blood (puṣṭaśoṇitāḥ): If a horse suffers from excess of blood in autumn season or from constipation in the rainy season,then clarified butter should be given to drink. In case of increase of kapha or vāyu, a horse should be given oil to drink.[26]

Disorder of vāyu, pitta and śleṣma: Dūrvā grass destroys pitta. Again yava would be favourable for cough. When a horse suffer from respiratory problem, arjuna would be beneficial. Moreover, diseases that are caused by disorder of vāyu, pitta and śleṣma would never attack a horse, who lives on a meal of dūrvā grass.[27]

Feeding and Nourishment:

Each meal of a horse should consist of four āḍhaka measures of yava, free of husks and mixed with caṇaka, vrīhi, mudga and kalāya pulse.[28] In case of Hoemoptysis (the coughing up of blood) in a horse, decoction of aśvakarṇa mixed with honey and sugar should be applied through its nostrils.On each seventh day salt should be given and vāruṇī wine should be given.[29] In autumn season, decoction of jīvanīya, mṛdvikā, sugar, honey, lotus petals, and pippalī should be given.[30] In winter season, liquid consists of viḍaṅga, pippalī, dhānya, śatāhvā, lodhra, saindhava salt and citraka should be given to drink.[31] In spring season, decoction of lodhra, priyaṅgu, musta, pippalī and viśva mixed with honey should be given to drink.This would also destroy kapha (phelgm).[32] In summer season, wine mixed with molasses and the juice of priyaṅgu, pippalī, lodhra and yaṣṭi should be given to drink and in rainy season, liquid consists of the essence of lodhra, kāṣṭha, pippalī, viśva and salt mixed with oil should be served.[33]

Preservation of Horse-Life:

References of shelters of elephants, stables of horses and references of chariots (hastyaśvarathaśālā) are met with in the Purāṇas.[34] While enumerating the ways of protecting the fort and the city of a king, the Matsya Purāṇa attests that the stables and cowsheds should be to the left side of the king’s palace. The stables should face to the north or to the south. If they face to other directions then it was considered bad. Moreover, there should be lamp burning in the stables through the whole night. The horses should stay in the stables.[35] It was customary to keep in the stables the following beasts for the welfare of the horses—the cocks, the monkeys, especially the apes, cows with their calves and she-goats. Dung should always be cleared away from the stables or elephant yards or cow sheds, before sunset. The quarters of the charioteers (sārathī) should be near the stables.[36] As diseases become more powerful in the fortress therefore, Veterinary doctors (govaidya, aśvavaidya and gajavaidya) should be provided lodging within the fortress.[37] It is suggested there in the Agni Purāṇa that, horses should be stabled in a well-ventilated house and dried bay should be strewn over its floors. The stable should be guarded well and lamps should be lighted there. Goats(aja), monkeys(kapaya), kṛkavākas, deers(mṛgas) should be kept in the stable.[38]

## 82. Part 19 - Horse in the Purāṇas—Introduction


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Horse in the Puranas—Introduction’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 19 - Horse in the Purāṇas—Introduction

The horse is said to have born of the Creator’s feet.[1] When the king Pṛthu, occupied the place of the presiding deity of the universe, at that time Hiraṇyagarbha Brahmā made Uccaiḥśravā the lord of horses, the bull the lord of cows and Gajendra Airāvata the lord of elephants.[2] Uccaiḥśravā, who came out of the churning of the ocean,[3] was the vehicle of the Devas.[4] The horse Uccaiḥśravā was of different colours, such as white, red, tawny, deer-coloured, green and grey.[5]

Breeds of Horses:

Four varieties of horses are mentioned in the Agni Purāṇa. They are—

1) Bhadra—the type of horse known as bhadra could be trained well.
2) Manda—the manda could be trained well by making use of a stick.
3) Mṛga—the type of horse known as mṛga is classified taking the shank of a deer as the basis, and
4) Saṅkīrṇa—the above characteristics would be found mixed in the saṅkīrṇa class.[6]

The Agni Purāṇa classifies the horses into four classes—

1) Brāhmaṇa—The horse that eats sugar, honey and fried rice and has a good smell is said to be clean and belonging to the brāhmaṇa class.
2) Kṣatriya—The horse that belongs to the kṣatriya class would be shiny, docile and intelligent.
3) Vaiśya—If the above qualities are found in lesser proportions it is known as the vaiśya class, and
4) Śūdra—The horse that is impure, unsteady, dull, ugly, foolish and wicked belongs to the śūdra class.[7]

The horses with [the following] signs are well known as pañcolūkhalikā [pañca-ulūkhalika].—

gomūtra,

kuṭilā,

veṇī,

padamaṇḍala and

mālikā.

These types of horses are excellent in their duties.[8]

Characteristics of Horses:

Various characteristics of horses are mentioned in the Purāṇas. Inauspicious and bad qualities are also mentioned there. According to the Agni Purāṇa, a horse with lesser number of teeth, one that is without teeth, one that is having shocking appearance, one that has a black sense of taste and black tongue, one that is born as a twin, one that has no testicle, one that has a cracked-hoof, one that has a horn, one that is tricoloured, one that has the colour of a tiger, an ass and ash, one that isn't having an excellent colour, one that has a lump, one that has white scrapes on the skin, one that is ridden by the crows, the voice of which looks like the voice of an ass, one whose eyes looks like the eyes of a monkey, one that has black manes, one whose anus is black, one whose nostrils have a black tone and are pointed, one that has the colour of a tittiri bird and one that has imbalanced legs having white scrapes on the foot,one that is without settled rings and has rings showing ominousness, ought to be avoided.[9]

Ten hair-whirls are said to be auspicious; two each at the skull and flanks. Two each at the head and at the chest, one each at the forehead and at the neck.[10] The whirls at the following places are considered most auspicious-at the ends of the mouth, at the forehead, at the root of the ear, at the throat, and at the roots of the arms; but at other places they are inauspicious.[11] The horses that are of the color of a parrot, the indragupa insects or the Moon, those that have colour like that of the crow, of the color of the hawk, and having a soft view are excellent.[12] Those having long necks, elongated eyes, prominent heads and short ears are good for the kings for victory at wars.[13] The horses that possess the above enumerated signs must be kept in the stud by which the offspring and possessions of its master would increase;otherwise misfortune would come to its master.[14] The horse is considered the holiest of the beasts. Therefore, it is sacrificed in the Aśvamedha.[15]

Various details are contemplated at various parts in the body of a horse, and in his other characteristics.[16] They are as follows:

a) In his mind the god Brahmā (brahmā citte),
b) In strength Viṣṇu (bale viṣṇuḥ),
c) In advances Garuḍa (vainateyaḥ parākrame)[17],
d) At the sides the Rudras (pārśve rudrā),
e) In intellect Guru (gururbuddhau),
f) At his vitals Viśvedevas (viśvedevāśca marmasu)[18],
g) At the whirl, at the curves of eyes the moon, at the sight the sun (dṛgāvarte dṛśīndvarkau),
h) At the ears Aśvins (karṇayoraśvinau tathā)[19],
i) At his stomach the Agni (jaṭhare’gniḥ),
j) In perspiration Svadhā (svadhā svede),
k) At the tongue Vāc (vāgjihvāyāṃ),
l) In the speed Anila (jave’nilaḥ)[20],
m) At his back the plain of heaven (pṛṣṭhato nākapṛṣṭhaḥ),
n) At the hooves all mountains(khurāgre sarvaparvatāḥ),[21]
o) At pores on his body the stars(tārāśca romakūpeṣu),
p) In heart the digit of the Moon (hṛdi cāndramasī kalā),[22] q) In ability Agni (tejasyagnī),
r) At the buttocks the goddess of love (ratiḥ śroṇyāṃ), s) At the fore-head the lord of the world (lalāṭe ca jagatpatiḥ),[23]
t) At his neigh the planets (grahāśca heṣite caiva) and
u) In the chest the chief of the serpents Vāsuki (tathaivorasi vāsukiḥ).[24]

Again, six kinds of phases of a horse are referred to in the Agni Purāṇa. They are—

saṃkṣipta,

vikṣipta,

kuñcita,

yathācita,

valgita and

avalgita[25]

Positions of Horses:

Various postures of horse are referred to in the Agni Purāṇa. They are—

1) Lokana—Having taken a firm seat on the saddle, the horse should be made to touch his legs with his face.This position is known as Lokanam.[26]

2) Udvakkana—Again, the posture known as the Udvakkanam consists in firmly clutching the two legs round the waist of a horse.[27]

3) Tāḍana—It is considered Tāḍanam if there is binding of the legs with the rein and letting it go by freeing the fore-legs.[28]

4) Moṭana—A wise man should know the loss of consciousness and destruction of a horse and the fourth rule known as the Moṭanam. A horse whose legs do not touch the ground in a small circle, that foot should be restrained by means of Moṭanam and Udvakkanam.[29]

5) Saṅgrahaṇa—It is said to be Saṅgrahaṇam when it is held with excertion and when one goes slowly holding it after fastening well on the seat.[30]

6) Grāhya-kaṇṭakapāyana—The posture known as Grāhya kaṇṭakapāyanam consists in hitting a horse in its flank and then holding the bridle with the feet.[31]

7) Hanana—Hananam consists in hitting with a stick, the leg of a horse, not making steps in a desired way, while running in one of the three motions, such as the trot, the gallop or the canter.[32]

8) Ucchvāsana—Ucchvāsanam consists in leading a horse to a different place, with its mouth restrained by means of rectangular bridle,[33] and

9) Mukhavyāvartana—It is considered Mukhavyāvartana, if the nature of a horse to turn his face and move towards the place from which it has been taken out, is restrained and held.[34]

After restraining by any one of the three ways in order, the horse should be controlled by taking to the courses such as the circles and the like in order.[35]

Favourable Period for Riding a Horse:

A horse should be first ridden under the influence of the asterisms aśvinī, hastā and the three uttarās.[36]

The seasons winter, the spring, and the forepart of the cold months, are the most favourable period to ride a horse, on the other hand in summer and autumn, a horse should not be harnessed or ridden upon—

hemantaḥ śiśiraścaiva vasantaścāścavāhane/ 
grīṣme śaradi varṣāsu niṣiddhaṃ vāhanaṃ haye//[37]

hemantaḥ śiśiraścaiva vasantaścāścavāhane/ 
grīṣme śaradi varṣāsu niṣiddhaṃ vāhanaṃ haye//[37]

## 83. Part 24 - Horses Used for Chariots


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Horses Used for Chariots’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 24 - Horses Used for Chariots

The Purāṇas refer to Rājapathas which were 10 dhanuṣas broad and which permitted easy movement for horses, chariots and elephants.[1]

The four horses of Kṛṣna’s chariot are:

Meghapuṣpa,

Śaibya,

Sugrīva and

Balāhaka.[2]

Bhauma’s (Mars) chariot was drawn by eight red horses.[3]

The seven metres of the Vedas were the seven horses of the Sun’s chariot.—

Gāyatrī,

Bṛhatī,

Uṣṇik,

Jagatī,

Triṣṭubh,

Anuṣṭubh and

Paṅkti.[4]

God Bṛhaspati had a golden chariot drawn by eight divine horses born of fire.[5]

In the Vāyu Purāṇa, [the following] are mentioned as the horses of the Moon’s chariot.—

Trimanā and

Turaṇya.[6]

[The following] were the names of the ten horses of the Moon’s chariot as mentioned in the Matsya Purāṇa.

Aja,

Tripatha,

Vṛṣa,

Vājī,

Nara,

Haya,

Aṃśumān,

Saptadhātu,

Haṃsa and

Vyomamṛga.[7]

Devadatta was the horse of Kalki.[8]

## 85. Part 25 - References of other Animals as found in the Purāṇas


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘References of other Animals as found in the Puranas’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 25 - References of other Animals as found in the Purāṇas

Buffalo:

We find in the Purāṇas, Mahiṣa i.e. buffalo as the riding animal of Yama, the god of death[1],as also of the Goddess Vārāhī.[2] According to the Matsya Purāṇa, Surabhi in union with the sage Kaśyapa, produced the buffaloes along with the cows.[3] The Vāyu Purāṇa speaks that Mṛgamandā (daughter of Krodhā and wife of Pulaha), gave birth to buffaloes, camels, pigs etc.[4] Offering of buffalo’s flesh satisfied the Pitṛs for ten months.[5] It was enjoined that in śrāddha rituals a she-buffalo should be given to Brāhmaṇa as she was the vehicle of Yama. This belief is vibrated in the Agni Purāṇa,[6] which adds that one’s desire is fulfilled and fortune is gained by giving the gift of a she-buffalo.

Camel:

Reference of Camels and mules are found in the Purāṇas.[7] Camel was used for chariot. Mahiṣāsura, an asura residing in Rasātala had a chariot drawn by camels.[8] The Matsya Purāṇa and the Viṣṇu Purāṇa states that Sugrīvī, the daughter of Tāmrā and Kaśyapa, was the mother of camels, sheep, goats, horses, and asses. They were the progenies of Tāmrā.[9]

Dog:

Śvānabali is an oblation to the two heavenly dogs Śyāma and Śabala. They were born in the family of Vaivasvata.Bali was offered to them in Yajña so that they may become non-violent.[10]

Donkey:

Reference of khara i.e. donkey[11] is also found. Donkey is shown, traditionally, to the left side of the idols of Kāma, the god of love.[12] Khara, donkey was produced from Sugrīvī.[13] Donkey or asses were also used for chariot. Kujambha, an asura, had a chariot drawn by donkeys.[14]

Goat:

Aja (goat) is born of the Creator’s mouth,[15] which is considered a useful animal in yajñas(as a victim)[16] along with cows, mahiṣas, aśvas, hastīs, and birds.

The goat, is the riding animal of Agni.[17] Its flesh pleases the Pitṛs for six months.[18] The mouth of the goat and horse was believed to be pure.[19] From the medicinal point of view the urine of a she goat is praised. According to the Agni Purāṇa the oil or the urine of a hornless goat is an excellent remedy for ear diseases.[20] The she goat’s milk is a remedy for bloody urine in case of a horse, if drunk on an empty stomach after getting boiled with sugar added and cooled.[21] The goat especially the she-goat is important in the ritual of the manes. The oblation to the manes may be given in fire or in the palm of a Brāhmaṇa, or in the ear of the she goat or that of the horse or in a cow-shed or near a water place,[22] but the milk of a she goat or a sheep was not to be offered to the manes.[23] According to the Kūrma Purāṇa the place where a śrāddha for the mane is performed has to be purified.For this purpose it is ordained that the place should be strewn with sesame seeds and goats should be tied all over the place; thereby everything polluted by the evils get purified.[24]

Sheep and Deer, as Riding Animal:

Śumbha, a commander of Tāraka’s force; had the gigantic black and white coloured sheep as his riding animal.[25] Deer (Mṛga) was the riding animal of the Goddess Patākinī.[26] Deer and other animals like hare were born of Mṛgī, who was the daughter of Krodhā and wife of Pulaha.[27] Again, Garuḍa was the riding animal of Goddess Vaiṣṇavī.[28]

## 87. Part 24 - Horses Used as Gifts


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Horses Used as Gifts’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 24 - Horses Used as Gifts

According to the Purāṇas the horses were also used as gifts. At the vow called Śarkarāsaptamī a horse made of gold worth 1,000 Niṣkas, 100 Niṣkas or 10 Niṣkas or even 5 Niṣkas, was to be donated.[1] He is said to be the very Viṣṇu in the form of the horse, the carrier of the Sun and the Moon.[2] The gift-horse made of gold was to be wrapped with a silk garment, and was to be presented to the preceptor.[3] For a king the gift of a horse-meru (aśva-meru) is prescribed. The Agni Purāṇa states that the aśva-meru consisting of fifteen or twelve horses should be donated after worshipping the gods.[4] Again the term Hiraṇyāśvaratha indicates the gift of a chariot with 7 or 8 horses in gold on an auspicious day. It keeps one away from hell and takes him to the abode of Śiva.[5] Hiraṇyaśva indicates the gift of a golden horse in an auspicious tithi leads one to the world of sun.[6]

## 88. Part 1 - Agriculture In Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Agriculture In Post-Puranic Sanskrit Works’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 1 - Agriculture In Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works

[Full title: Agriculture in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas]

In this chapter the study is made on some of the major post-Purāṇic Sanskrit works written on agriculture and animal husbandry. The relevance of Purāṇic agriculture and animal husbandry with the modern system of agriculture and animal husbandry will also be discussed here.

The source materials of this chapter which are found to be post-Purāṇic are listed below:

1) Arthaśāstra of Kauṭilya (321-300 B.C.)
2) Bṛhatsaṃhitā of Varāhamihira(c. sixth century A.D.)
3) Kāśyapīyakṛṣisūkti of Kāśyapa(c. 700-800 A.D.)
4) Kṛṣi-parāśara of Parāśara (c. A.D. 950)
5) Śukranītisāra of Śukra (c. fourteenth to sixteenth century A.D.)
6) Upavanavinoda of Śārṅgadhara (c. fourteenth century A.D.)
7) Viśvavallabha of Chakrapāṇi Miśra(1577 A.D.)
8) Vṛkṣāyurveda of Surapāla (c. tenth century A.D.)

In the Arthaśāstra, Kauṭilya terms agriculture, breeding of cattle and trade as Vārtā.[1] Kāśyapa in his Kāśyapīyakṛṣisūkti opines that production of grains and other vegetation are the only purpose for highest achievement on the earth. The rich earth full of vegetation is the reason of development of living beings.[2] According to Kāśyapa, by producing grains and medicinal plants and by the water which springs from its internal, earth is beneficial to the life of all beings.

So earth is also called as Medinī—

dhānyādīnāmoṣadhīnāṃ jalānāṃ prasravādapi/ 
dharā tu medinī khyātā prāṇināṃ prāṇavardhinī //[3]

dhānyādīnāmoṣadhīnāṃ jalānāṃ prasravādapi/ 
dharā tu medinī khyātā prāṇināṃ prāṇavardhinī //[3]

Kāśyapa has argued the profession of farming as a noble one. He states that the occupation of farming is commendable which gives delight in numerous ways.

Any other livelihood involves dependence.[4] According to Kāśyapa, agriculture is the highest of all wealth.[5] Yielding profuse returns, farming provides pure grains, and other things which please gods.[6] That’s why, qualified persons through their own efforts, being truthful and kind hearted, always engage them in agriculture.[7] It is found in the Kāśyapīyakṛṣisūkti that people of all castes were engaged to practice agriculture.[8] In the post-Purāṇic Sanskrit works, the process of agriculture can be sub divided under the following heads:

## 89. Part 2 - Ploughing for Sowing Seeds


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Ploughing for Sowing Seeds’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 2 - Ploughing for Sowing Seeds

So many procedures of sowing seeds have been pointed out in the Kāśyapīyakṛṣisūkti. In the śālī fields, ploughing by auspicious bullocks has been directed.[1] In pleasure grove, gardens, and royal parks, either on the outskirts or inside of villages and towns, ploughing of land by oxen, leveling with the axes and spades, adjustment of fields with the paring plow has been intended.[2] The experts in agriculture have advised ploughing, furrowing, or digging for the sowing of seeds.[3] Ploughing of land in the places for growing creepers is recommended. A farmer should undertake ploughing, digging of pits, and furrowing in the ground which is selected for growing vegetables, gardens or trees.[4] In planting creepers and trees, in placing the kadalī roots and in planting sprouts, one should organize all the agricultural operations such as ploughing etc. to get better results.[5] An intelligent agriculturist should begin the process of farming on an auspicious time at the beginning of the year.[6] Land should be first ploughed in the auspicious north east corner.[7] Kāśyapa refers to worship of plough as well as bullocks.[8] It is there mentioned that worship of the field or land yields good results.[9] Kāśyapa enjoins that bullocks must not be made to overwork.[10] He further asks pardon for any injuries made to Goddess Earth during plowing and prays for immense yields in the field.[11] The Purāṇas also lay down such practice of ploughing.

The Bhaviṣya Purāṇa enjoins that after the process of ploughing, grains of paddy are to be sowed. It is also enjoined that, after ploughing and the grains are sown, gold dust(suvarṇa-mṛttikā) should be sprinkled in the central place.[12] The purity which is achieved by ploughing is mentioned in the Purāṇas. It is stated that by different process of agriculture such as ploughing the fields, cutting the herbs on the soil, the tillers get rid of sin and achieve purity by pacifying the gods through sacrifice.[13]

## 90. Part 3 - Sowing of Seeds


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Sowing of Seeds’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 3 - Sowing of Seeds

In the Vṛkṣāyurveda, Surapāla has mentioned that one should become pure before starting the process of sowing seeds in the land or farm. The owner of the land should have bath at the very first. After that becoming pure and wearing clean garments he should worship gods and offer respect to Vasumatī. After performing this ritualistic procedure with devotion one should sow the seeds in the ground.[1] In the Upavana-Vinoda it is mentioned that, one should sow pulses and sesamum on a level ground after it has been thoroughly ploughed with a plough, and should cut the crops when ripe and then would sow seeds at that ground again.[2]

The Upavana-Vinoda too suggests that a man should sow seeds after putting clean clothes and after worshipping gods, making obeisance to spiritual gods and to the presiding deity of the foundation—

śucisnāto vibhradbacanamamalaṃ pūjitasuro/
guruṃ natvā dattvā vasu vasumatīṃ vā guṇavate/
svayaṃ bījānyādau vapati katicidvāstupuruṣaṃ
manasyantaḥ kṛtvā tadanu paritonyaḥ parijanaḥ//[3]

śucisnāto vibhradbacanamamalaṃ pūjitasuro/
guruṃ natvā dattvā vasu vasumatīṃ vā guṇavate/
svayaṃ bījānyādau vapati katicidvāstupuruṣaṃ
manasyantaḥ kṛtvā tadanu paritonyaḥ parijanaḥ//[3]

Kauṭilya in his Arthaśāstra mentions that the superintendent of agriculture should employ labors to sow seeds after thoroughly ploughing the lands.[4] According to Kauṭilya, the superintendent of agriculture, possessed of the knowledge of the science of agriculture, dealing with the plantation of bushes and trees or assisted by those who are educated in such sciences, should collect the seeds of all kinds of grains, flowers, fruits, vegetables, bulbous roots, fibre-producing plants and cotton etc. in time.[5] Parāśara says that seeds that are to be sown on the same field, all of them should be of the same variety, because the seeds which are of the same class, yield a prosperous harvest.[6] The Purāṇas also refer to sowing of auspicious seeds, which comprise yava, śāli-rice, tila, mudga, godhūma and śvetasarṣapa(white mustard), kulattha and māṣa. It is suggested that these seeds should be well washed before sowing.[7] A śarāva (earthen saucer), a small water-pot and a small raised bank or embankments are the most convenient post or places for growing on the above seeds.[8]

## 91. Part 4 - Treatment of Seeds


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Treatment of Seeds’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 4 - Treatment of Seeds

The Vṛkṣāyurveda suggests the methods for making the seeds more viable. In the first method the seeds are said to be sprinkled with milk and then cow-dung should be pasted.Then paste containing honey and viḍaṅga powder should be rubbed on them.[1] In a different procedure, it is said that the seeds should be soaked in milk (kṣīreṇa bhāvitam) and then should be dried up. After that, powder consists of sarṣapa, vṛhatī, tila and nāla should be pasted. Thus, the seeds should be treated for perfect germination and for making them viable.[2] The seeds of mākanda, jambu and panasa if treated with this method, become better viable.[3] The seeds treated with proper procedures following the process previously described, become perfect for sowing and produce flowers and fruits in ample quantity.[4] The Bṛhatsaṃhitā too mentions about the treatment of seeds. According to Bṛhatsaṃhitā any seeds firstly soaked in milk for ten days, then taking them out daily with the hand smeared with ghee. After that they must be rolled many times in cow dung, there after they must be fumigated with the flesh of deer and hog; there upon with flesh and hog’s marrow, they should be then planted in a soil which was already prepared with sowing of sesamum. Seeds treated thus, and sprinkled with milk and water will grow and flourish.[5]

Cakrapāṇi Miśra also mentions in Viśvavallabha that seeds should be treated with dried cow dung and then with clarified butter and honey. After rubbing them in dung or after smearing them with a paste of ashes of tila plants and seeds and clarified butter, one should sow the seeds at proper places.[6] Śārṅgadhara in the Upavana-Vinoda states that, one should smear the seeds sprinkled with milk, with powders of vṛhatī and sesamum mixed with ghee. Then the seeds should be dried. After that one should besmear the seeds with cow dung and then fumigate with fat of some animal. If the seeds are sown after treated thus, they would sprout within a night.[7]

## 92. Part 5 - Successful Production Depends on the Fertility of the Soil


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Successful Production Depends on the Fertility of the Soil’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 5 - Successful Production Depends on the Fertility of the Soil

Kāśyapa points out the importance of good soil stating thus-a good quality land yields good results to everyone, confers good health on the family and causes growth of money, cattle and grain.[1]

He further mentions that a good soil—

(i) should be devoid of bones and stones,[2]  
(ii) should be a plastic clay, with reddish and black colour, full of potency and glossy with water,[3]
(iii) should not be too deep or shallow,[4]
(iv) should be conducive to speedy seedling emergence,[5]
(v) should be easy for ploughing and other operations,[6]
(vi) should easily absorb moisture and should be inhabited with beneficial living creatures;[7] and
(vii) should have a substantial mass.[8]

With regard to the fertility of the soil, it is mentioned in the Bhāgavata Purāṇa that if the seeds are sown in the same plot of land, time and again without manuring, the land would lose its fertility and consequently the seeds would get destroyed[9].

Cakrapāṇi Miśra in his Viśvavallabha mentions three types of soil-arid, wet and ordinary. It is differentiated on the basis of six types of taste.[10]

According to him soil is of six colours—

dark gray,

pallid,

black,

white,

red and

yellow.

Their tastes are-sweet, sour, saline, bitter, pungent and astringent respectively.[11] The land which is soft and water is available nearby is good for plantation. Therefore, gardens should be planned on such locations.[12]

Śārṅgadhara in the Upavana-Vinoda too mentions three main types of soil, viz.

Jāṅgala (barren),

Anūpa (moist) and

Sāmānya (ordinary).

They are divided into six on the basis of colour and taste.[13] Soils having black, pale, blue, red, yellow and white colour are correlated with sweet, acid, salty, sour, bitter and pungent tastes respectively.[14] All kinds of trees flourish in the soil, near which there is water and where the sprouts of trees put on green appearance.[15]

The Agni Purāṇa also mention various types of grounds. They are—

white coloured,

blood coloured,

blood smelling ground and

yellow coloured ground.[16]

Again [in the Agni Purāṇa, the following] are also mentioned.—

golden soil,

sandy soil,

blue soil,

yellow soil,

white soil and

yellowish red soil.[17]

## 93. Part 6 - The modern relevance of ancient Indian Manuring


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘modern relevance of ancient Indian Manuring’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 6 - The modern relevance of ancient Indian Manuring

Parāśara gives importance in the cattle manure for the stable and suitable growth of paddy. He says that without the application of manure the paddy plants do not give fruits, they only simply grow up.[1] Parāśara recommends the methods how dung is to be carefully maintained, stored up, crushed and at last applied to the field.[2]  Kauṭilya also refers to the use of manure in the process of agriculture. He states that sprout of seeds when grown are to be manured with a fresh hall of tiny fishes and should irrigated with the milk of snuhi (Euphorbia antiquorum).[3] Kāśyapa emphasizes in application of manures at proper time[4] which included cow-dung, goat-dung, and compost.[5] Cakrapāṇi Miśra in his Viśvavallabha mentions that all trees produce sweet fruits when sprinkled with milk.[6] It is suggested to smear the branches, roots and trunks of trees with cow dung and plant them in pits filled with soil and to sprinkle dried dung abundantly with water.[7] The Purāṇas mention the using of manure in the fields to increase the fertility of the soil as well as for the growth of crops. The Agni Purāṇa mentions that to increase the production of flowers and fruits, one should sprinkle ghee with cold milk.[8] It further mentions that maturing the soil with powdered barley, sesamum and the powder of dried dung of a goat, mixed together and soaked in meat and water for seven continuous nights is good for the increase of flowers and fruits.[9]

## 94. Part 7 - Irrigation and Water Management


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Irrigation and Water Management’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 7 - Irrigation and Water Management

A tree should be planted in the morning and evening if it does not rain.[1] In rainy season plants should be watered at mid day. In winter season they should be watered in the morning and in the spring season they should be watered in the afternoon and in the summer season trees should be watered in the evening.[2] In the Vṛkṣāyurveda[3] and Upavana-Vinoda[4] mention is made about Kuṇapajala—the solution for nourishing, which is very healthy for plants. One should boil the flesh, fat and marrow of deer, pig, fish, goat and rhinoceros in water. When it is properly boiled then the mixture should be put in an earthen pot and milk, powder of sesamum oil cakes, māṣa boiled in honey, the decoction of pulses, clarified butter and hot water should be added there to. When this earthen pot is put in a warm place for a fortnight the compound becomes what is known as Kuṇapajala.[5]

In the Kṛṣi-parāśara, Parāśara gives special stress in the conservation of water in the fields from mid-September to mid-November—

āśvine kārtike caiva dhānyasya jalarakṣaṇam/
na kṛtaṃ yena mūrkhena tasya kā sasyavāsanā//[6]

āśvine kārtike caiva dhānyasya jalarakṣaṇam/
na kṛtaṃ yena mūrkhena tasya kā sasyavāsanā//[6]

He opines that water is essential to crops as the women are to the families.[7] Kauṭilya in his Arthaśāstra, gives importance on rainfall for the support of vegetation. He says that when one third of the requisite rain falls during the beginning and the closing months of the rainy season(the month of Śrāvaṇa and Kārttika) and two-third in the middle month(Proṣṭhapada and Āśvayuja) then that rainfall was considered normal.[8] According to Kauṭilya a forecast of rainfall can be made by observing the position and motion of Jupiter, the rise and motion of Venus and the aspects of Sun.[9] The superintendent of agriculture shall sow the seeds which require either more or less water, according to the availability of the rainfall which may be more or less.[10] Śāli, vrīhi, kodrava, tila, priyaṅgu, dāraka and varaka are to be sown at the beginning of the rainy season. Mudga, māṣa and śaibya are to be sown at the middle and kusumbha, masūra, kulattha,yava, godhūma, atasī and sarṣapa are to be sown at last.[11]

In the Viśvavallabha, constructions of water reservoirs are found.

Cakrapāṇi Miśra recommends four types of ponds, viz.

Nandā,

Bhadrā,

Jayā and

Vijayā.[12]

Seven kinds of wells have been mentioned. They are—

Vijaya,

Dundubhi,

Cūḍāmaṇi,

Bhadra,

Jaya,

Nanda and

Śaṃkara.[13]

Potholes, where rain water accumulated naturally were of four kinds, viz.—

Bhadra,

Subhadra,

Parighra, and

Nanda.[14]

Techniques were used to keep water of wells safe using herbs or other materials. Even brackish, foul and turbid water was treated.[15] The Upavana-Vinoda too mentions the procedure of watering plants. It states that one should water the newly planted trees every alternate day in autumn and in winter seasons; every day in spring season and two times a day during summer season.[16] The Purāṇas too gave importance on rainfall for the growth of crops. The Matsya Purāṇa states that agricultural prospects flourish by rain which again depends on fixed time.[17] The Agni Purāṇa mentions that the god Parjanya, Arka and Indu,should be worshipped to guard against any disturbance that affects the annual rainfall of a country.[18] It is also recommended in the Purāṇas that the fort of a king should be situated in a country watered by large rivers not depending upon the atmospheric causes for its water -supply.[19]

## 95. Part 8 - Protection and Care (of crops)


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Protection and Care (of crops)’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 8 - Protection and Care (of crops)

Adequate attention is given to the protection and care of crops. The plantation (vṛkṣaropaṇaṃ) according to Surapāla is recognized as a source for attaining the puruṣārtha catuṣṭaya i.e. dharma, artha, kāma and mokṣa.[1] Rainy season is the best time for sowing all types of seeds and planting trees, etc. After rains the seeds should be planted. Autumn and spring season are average seasons for the purpose.[2] Winter and summer are both contraindicated for plantation. By the way all seasons are equally good for plantation if extra efforts are given on watering.[3] Kauṭilya states that the sprouting of seeds can be inferred from the Sun, the formation of grain can be inferred from the position of Jupiter, and rainfall can be inferred from the movement of Venus.[4]

On the occasion of the sowing of seeds for the first time a handful of the seeds bathed in water with a piece of gold should be sown and the following mantra should be recited—

prajāpataye kāśyapāya devāya namaḥ sadā/ 
sītā me ṛdhyatāṃ devī bījeṣu ca dhaneṣu ca//

prajāpataye kāśyapāya devāya namaḥ sadā/ 
sītā me ṛdhyatāṃ devī bījeṣu ca dhaneṣu ca//

In this mantra, salutation is given to the divine Kāśyapa and it is prayed that agriculture may flourish always and the goddess may reside in seeds and wealth.[5]

Reference of such recitation of mantra written in leaf is also met with in the Agni Purāṇa. According to it the following mantra written on a leaf, amidst the heap of rice under the influence of asterisms known as the three Pūrvās i.e., Pūrvaphālgunī, Pūrvāṣāḍhā, Pūrvabhādrapada, Viśākhā, Dhaniṣṭhā and Śatabhiṣā gives increase in the rice—[6]

dhanadāya sarvadhaneśāya dehi me dhanaṃ svāhā/
nave harṣe ilādevī lokasaṃvardhini kāmarūpiṇi dehi me dhanaṃ svāhā//

dhanadāya sarvadhaneśāya dehi me dhanaṃ svāhā/
nave harṣe ilādevī lokasaṃvardhini kāmarūpiṇi dehi me dhanaṃ svāhā//

The Bṛhatsaṃhitā devotes a chapter to the growth of crops.[7] It states that when the sun enters into Vṛścika, Jupiter be posited in Kumbha, the Moon in Vṛṣabha, Mars and Saturn in Makara then there is great prosperity of corn.[8] Again when Venus or Mercury, or both, are posited in the second house from the Sun (in Vṛścika), or similarly in the twelfth house, summer corn will grow well.[9] When Venus, the Moon and Mercury are posited in their order in the 11th, 4th and 2nd from the Sun in Vṛścika, corn will thrive well. If in the above Yoga, Jupiter is posited in the 10th, great prosperity of cattle is also assured.[10] The Upavana-Vinoda mentions that trees get diseases through the affections of vāta, pitta and kapha. One should diagnose the diseases through their symptoms and cure them radically.[11]

## 96. Part 9 - Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works—Introduction


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Animal Husbandry in Post-Puranic Sanskrit Works—Introduction’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 9 - Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works—Introduction

[Full title: Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas—Introduction]

According to Kauṭilya, agriculture, cattle-breeding was the occupation for all classes of people.[1] As it gives grains and cattle, forests produce and free labour, so it is most useful.[2] Animals were used for various purposes. It is noticed that different animal products like pig’s fat, cow-dung, cow-bones etc. were required for fertilizing seeds or other parts of plants as pre-plantation treatment. Broad use of animal bones was required for this purpose.[3] Requirement of animal power (bullock) for raising water from well, for irrigation is also noticed.[4] The Bṛhatsaṃhitā and the Vṛkṣāyurveda mentions extensive use of various products of different species of animals in nourishment of plants. The Bṛhatsaṃhitā suggests that two āḍhakas (128 palas) of the powder of the dung of goats and sheep, one āḍhaka of sesamum, a prastha (16 palas) of wheat particles, a tulā (100 palas) of beef and a droṇa (256 palas) of water kept for seven nights should be sprinkled always for increasing the yield of flowers and fruits of trees, creepers and shrubs etc.[5] The Vṛkṣāyurveda mentions that the seeds of good quality (sādhuvīja) of kūṣmāṇḍa, vārttāka, paṭola and other vegetable fruits should be soaked in majjā of female beer (kroḍī) and after that should be sown in land. For irrigation, the water mixed with same animal majjā as stated above should be used. This produces fruits without seeds.[6]

## 97. Part 10 - Breeds of Animals (in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works)


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 10 - Breeds of Animals (in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works)

[Full title: Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas (1) Breeds of Animals]

The Arthaśāstra mentions different kinds of domesticated animals such as horse, donkey, camel, buffalo, goat, sheep and pig etc.[1] The horses belonging to the breed of Kamboja, Sindhu, Araṭṭa and Vanāyu countries were the best horses for use in battle etc; those of Vāhlīka, Pāpeya, Sauvīra and Taitala region were of middle quantity. The rest of the breeds were considered ordinary.[2] Regarding the quality of horses it is mentioned in the Śukranītisāra that those horses are good which run with legs thrown from a height, whose movements are like those of tigers, peacocks, ducks, parrots, pigeons, deer, camels, monkeys and bulls.[3]

In case of bulls it is stated by Śukra that the bull which is neither idle nor wicked but good for carrying load with a well-formed body and a good back is the best of all.[4] Reference of camels is also met with in the Śukranītisāra. It is mentioned there that the camel which is strong-built, has a good face, carries burden and goes thirty yojanas (1 yojana=4 miles[5]) a day is appreciated.[6] The bull and the camels should be governed by strings with which the nose can be pulled.[7]

The Śukranītisāra mentions four classes of elephants and their characteristics. The four classes according to Śukranītisāra are—

Bhadra,

Manda,

Mṛga and

Miśra.[8]

The elephants whose tusks are like the colour of honey, which are strong and well-formed, round and fat in body, have good face and excellent limbs are called as Bhadra class of elephants.[9] The elephant which has fat belly, lion-like eyes, thick skin, throat and trunk, medium limbs and a long body is known as Manda elephant.[10] The Mṛga elephant is that which has small or short throat, tusks, ears and trunk, big eyes and very short lips.[11] The Miśra elephant has the above said characteristics in mixture.[12]

The Arthaśāstra classifies elephants into four groups according to the training they are given. They are:

1) the elephants which are tameable (damya);
2) the elephants which are trained for war (sānnāhya);
3) the elephants which are trained for riding (aupavāhya); and
4) the rogue elephants (vyāla).[13]

According to the Arthaśāstra, the elephant which is seven aratnis in height, nine aratnis in length, ten aratnis in circumference, and is 40 years old is considered the best.[14] The Bṛhatsaṃhitā mentions the signs of cows[15], dogs[16], goats[17], horses[18] and elephants[19] in details. In the Purāṇas too we find the references of breeds of animals such as horses, cows, bulls, elephants etc.

In case of horses four varieties are mentioned. They are—

bhadra,

manda,

mṛga and

saṅkīrṇa.[20]

In case of elephants also there are four varieties. They are mentioned in the Matsya Purāṇa as—

Kumuda,

Añjana,

Vāmana and

Airāvata.[21]

Various types of bulls are mentioned. They are—

Nandīmukha,

Samudra and

Nīla Vṛṣabha.[22]

Moreover, mention of goats, sheep, buffaloes, camels, dogs is met with in the Purāṇas.

Animal Breeding:

The people fully knew the necessities of number of males of different animal species per herd of one hundred females of different types of domestic animals for the purpose of breeding. Kauṭilya in his Arthaśāstra mentions that in a herd of one hundred asses and mules there shall be five male animals and in the case of herds of goats and sheep there shall be ten males per hundred. For a herd of ten heads of cows or buffaloes there shall be four male animals each.[23]

## 98. Part 11 - Rearing and Tending of Animals


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Rearing and Tending of Animals’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 11 - Rearing and Tending of Animals

[Full title: Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas (2) Rearing and Tending of Animals]

Rearing of animals of State’s property was carried under the office of superintendents for separate species of domestic animals.[1] It is suggested in the Arthaśāstra that cattle rearers shall apply remedies to calves and aged cows which suffer from various diseases.[2] Cattle of ten similar colour are grouped at the time of grazing. Cowherds shall take the cattle to graze in pastures either far or near according to their ability to protect them.[3] Cowherds shall graze the cattle in forest regions which are allotted as pasture grounds for the purpose of grazing for various seasons and which are protected from thieves and other molesting beasts by hunters.[4] A pony after the tenth day of its birth shall be given kuḍaba flowers mixed with one-fourth quantity of clarified butter and one prastha milk till it becomes six months old. During each following month the above rations should be increased half as much, with the addition of one prastha of barley till it becomes three years old, and thereafter with the addition of one droṇa of barley till it grows four years old. At the age of four or five it attains full development.[5]

The Arthaśāstra mentions the group of persons to attend the needs of the elephants. They are—

cikitsaka (elephant doctor),

anīkastha (trainer),

ārohaka (expert rider),

ādhoraṇa (who grooms them),

hastipaka (the protector),

aupacārika (one who massages),

vidhāpācaka (the cook),

yāvasika (one who procures grass for them),

pādapāśika (those who has tighten them to posts),

kuṭīrakṣaka (the sweeper of the stable) and

aupaśāyika (one who keeps watch in the stables at night).[6]

It is also advised there that the elephant doctors shall apply necessary medicines to elephants, while making a journey or suffer from diseases, over work or old age.[7]

The Śukranītisāra suggests some points that are to be noticed in case of managing a horse. One should strike the horse at the breast if it is terrified, at the neck if it neighs, at the posterior if it slips, at the mouth if going astray, at the tail if it gets angry, at the belly if it is absent minded.[8] It is also prohibited to strike the horse very often and at wrong places.[9] It is suggested there that one should ride the horse in the morning and evening in October and November, winter and spring, in the evening in summer, in the morning in autumn.[10] One should not use the horse in the rainy season or on uneven grounds.[11]

Reference of rearing and tending of animals are found in the Purāṇas. It is pointed out in the Matsya Purāṇa that a king’s Commander-in-chief should be expert in managing horses and elephants.[12] Regarding horse-care, it is said that the groom of a king should know about horses and how to treat the horses when ill.[13] A horse should be first ridden under the influence of the asterisms aśvinī, hastā and the three uttarās.[14] The seasons winter, the spring, and the forepart of the cold months, are the most favourable to ride a horse, on the other hand in summer and autumn, a horse should not be harnessed nor ridden upon.[15]

## 99. Part 12 - Nourishment and Feeding of Animals


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Nourishment and Feeding of Animals’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 12 - Nourishment and Feeding of Animals

[Full title: Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas (3) Nourishment and Feeding of Animals]

It is recommended in the Arthaśāstra, for horses, the diet of two droṇas of any one of the grains like rice (śālī, vrīhi), barley, panic seeds (priyaṅgu) soaked or cooked, cooked mudga (Phaseolus mango) or māṣa (Phaseolus Radiatus), and one prastha of oil would be best. Five palas of salt and fifty palas of flesh are to be added therewith. One āḍhaka of broth (rasa) or two āḍhakas of curd is needed to soften this food item. Five palas of sugar, one prastha of liquor (surā) and two prasthas of milk are needed to make the above diet savoring.[1]

For bulls which are equal to horses in speed and in carrying loads, [the following] will form the diet.[2]—

half a bhāra of meadow grass (yavasa),
twice bhāra of ordinary grass (tṛṇa),
one tulā (100 palas) of oil cakes,
10 āḍhakas of bran,
five palas of salt (mukhalavaṇam),
one kuḍuba of oil for rubbing over the nose,
1 prastha of drink (pāna),
one tulā of flesh,
1 āḍhaka of curds,
one droṇa of barley or cooked māṣa (Phaselous radiatus),
one droṇa of milk or half an āḍhaka of surā (liquor),
1 prastha of oil or ghee (sneha),
10 palas of sugar or jaggery,
1 pala of the fruit of śṛṅgivera (ginger),

The same commodities less by one quarter each will form the diet for mules, cows and asses; twice the quantity of the above things for buffaloes and camels. Draught oxen and cows, supplying milk, should be provided, twice the quantity as said above, according to the duration of time the oxen are kept at work and the quantity of milk which the cow supplies. All the cattle shall be supplied with abundance of fodder and water.[3]

The shares for an elephant (of seven aratnis in height) should be—

one droṇa of rice,
half an āḍhaka of oil,
3 prasthas of ghee,
10 palas of salt,
50 palas of flesh,
one āḍhaka of broth (rasa), or two āḍhakas of curd (in order to tender the dish tasteful),
10 palas of sugar,
one āḍhaka of liquor, or two āḍhakas of milk.

One prastha of oil is required for smearing over the body. Moreover, one-eight prastha of oil for applying on the head and for use in a lamp is to be kept in the stables.[4] The Śukranītisāra too mentions that the horse should be made to take milk, ghee, water and powdered grains.[5] The horse should be given peas or grains, māṣa, mudga, both dry and wet, as well as well-cooked meat.[6] The best foods for horses are yava and caṇaka; māṣa and makuṣṭhaka are considered good and masūra and mudga are considered inferior stuff.[7]

The Purāṇas too mention some diets for animals. It is recommended there that a calf should be given a food which consist of māṣa, tila, godhūma, payaḥ, kṣīra and ghṛta(clarified butter), mixed with lavaṇa, to develop well.[8] The Agni Purāṇa states that to give a morsel of grass to the cows(go-grāsa) was considered the daily duties of a householder. After go-grāsa and performing the ritual svastyayana and after worshipping the guests, the householder should take his food.[9] In case of diet of elephant it is said that the food which consists of ṣaṣṭi, vrīhi and śālī rice, is considered the best food for an elephant.[10] The food consists of wheat and barley should be ranked as second best, while the other sorts of food should be considered inferior sort.[11]

## 100. Part 14 - Agriculture in the Modern Age—Introduction


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Agriculture in the Modern Age—Introduction’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 14 - Agriculture in the Modern Age—Introduction

[Full title: Agriculture in the Modern Age of 20-21st Century]

The Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research (CGIAR) states that sustainable agriculture is the successful management of resources to satisfy the quality of environment and conserving natural resources.[1] There is no doubt that the ancient treatises like the Vedas as well as the Purāṇas are the origin of agriculture and animal husbandry. The modern system of agriculture and animal husbandry has definitely inherited the process of the same from the Vedas and the Purāṇas. Obviously there may be some changes in the traditional agricultural system; some processes may be excluded from the indigenous system of agriculture and some different processes may be added to the modern agricultural system. But we cannot disregard our traditional knowledge system regarding agriculture and animal husbandry which is still prevailing in most of the parts of our country. The indigenous system of agriculture and animal husbandry has been achieving value day by day. The modern agriculturalists have improved these traditional methods gradually by applying science and technologies in the field of agriculture as well as in animal husbandry. So difference is observed in the system of agriculture of modern periods with that of the ancient texts. We have to give a look into the modern system of agriculture and animal husbandry to find out its relevance with the Purāṇic system. In the direction of modern agricultural research the establishment of Imperial Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR) in 1929, was the most significant step.[2]

## 101. Part 13 - Preservation of Animal Life


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Preservation of Animal Life’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 13 - Preservation of Animal Life

[Full title: Animal Husbandry in Post-Purāṇic Sanskrit Works and its Relevance with the Purāṇas (4) Preservation of Animal Life]

Avoidance of cruelty towards draught animals (carriage horses) and milch-animals (particularly cattle) is noticed by specification of the distance to be traversed[1] and number of milking times in a day in different seasons respectively. It is recommended that cattle may be milked twice a day (morning and evening) during the season of rain, autumn and winter. In the seasons of later part of winter, spring and summer milking may be limited to one time (in the morning alone).[2] This is also suggested that sheep and other animals should be shorn of their wool once in six months.[3] Any kind of physical injury to livestock was liable to punishment.[4]

The superintendent should make a stable which is twice as broad as the length of a horse. It should be constructed as spacious as required by the number of horses to be kept therein, with four doors facing the four quarters. Moreover, it should be contained monkeys, peacocks, red-spotted deer, mongoose, partridge, parrots and mainā birds.[5] The stables of elephants should be constructed twice as broad, twice as high as the length (āyāma) of an elephant. The apartments for female elephants should be separate. There should be posts in the stable called kumārī to tie the elephants. The door of the stable should be facing either the east or the north.[6] The Purāṇas too suggest some ways for preservation of animal life. The method of fumigation of a cow house with the vapours of viṣā plants for making the cattle strong is noticed in the Agni Purāṇa.[7] Again it is advised that a fumigation of the cow-house with the vapours of devadāru, vacā, māṃsī, guggula, hiṅgu and sarṣapa mixed together would keep safe from the evil effects of malignant planets.[8]

## 102. Part 15 - Major crops and Staple food


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Major crops and Staple food’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 15 - Major crops and Staple food

[Full title: Agriculture in the Modern Age of 20-21st Century (1) Major Crops]

Rice is the staple food of nearly three-fourth of the population in India. Its cultivation goes back to the unrecorded ages extending beyond 3,000 B. C. The customary use of rice in the religious ceremonies of the Hindus, which are associated with birth, marriage and death, is evidence to its great antiquity.[1] In India, generally three crops of paddy are raised in a year: autumn, winter and summer. Of these, the winter crop is the most important, reporting for about 90 percent of the total; the autumn crop accounts for less than 10 percent, and the summer crop constitutes the negligible balance.

The sowing and harvesting seasons of the three crops are as follows:[2]

Other food crops grown in India are wheat, millet, barley, maize, pulses, potato, etc. Various kind of oilseeds such as ground nut, mustard, sesamum, castor, linseed, safflower, etc. and fibre crops such as cotton, jute etc. are also grown in India. In Assam, among the cereals, rice is the single most important crop followed very remotely by wheat and maize.[3]

Agriculture in Assam depends upon rainfall. For this reason and for the agro-climatic difference, in Assam, there are three distinct rice-growing seasons. They are:

1) Śāli or winter rice(June/July to November/December);
2) Āhu or autumn rice(March/April to June/July) and
3) Boro or summer rice(November/December to May/June).[4]

Major pulses grown in Assam and adjoining north-eastern states are black gram (Vigna mungo) and green gram (Vigna radiata) during kharif and summer seasons and field pea (Pisum sativum), lentil(Lens culinaris) and lathyrus(Lathyrus sativus) during rabi seasons.[5]

In recent years the following plant species were introduced into India[6] :

1) Sunflower (Helianthus annuus); 
2) Tree-tomato (Cyphomandra betacea); 
3) Spearmint (Mentha arvensis); 
4) Kiwifruit (Actindia chinensis); 
5) Gum-arabic (Acacia senegal);

In the Purāṇas also various kinds of grains are mentioned. People in the days of the Purāṇas could recognize the crops. Mention of seventeen kinds of grāmya oṣadhis and fourteen kinds of yajñīya oṣadhis is met with in the Purāṇas.[7] Some of the agricultural crops mentioned in the Purāṇas are-tila[8] dhānya[9] (eighteen sorts of grain), vrīhi[10] (a kind of paddy), śyāmāka[11] saktu[12] (the flour of grain), nīvāra[13] (a kind of corn for śrāddha), māṣa[14] (a kind of grain), masūra, yava and godhūma[15] etc.

## 103. Part 16 - Tools Used for Agriculture


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Tools Used for Agriculture’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 16 - Tools Used for Agriculture

[Full title: Agriculture in the Modern Age of 20-21st Century (2) Tools Used for Agriculture]

It is established that the harvesting of the different crops is carried out at present only by physical work and the implements used are only normal sickles, the cutting edge of which is specially hollowed out for this reason.[1]

So the preliminary tillage may be carried out by following three methods—

(a) By the manual labour of digging the ground with hand devices,
(b) By ploughing with bullocks or other draught animals, and
(c) By mechanical power.[2]

Moreover, threshing machines, winnowing machines, feed cutting machines, stone threshing roller, wheat threshing roller etc. are also used. In modern times, tractors are being used for farming processes in India.

The scope for tillage with tractors and tractor-drawn implements lie mostly in three ways, viz.

(1) For clearing of scrub jungle or waste land to be newly brought under cultivation;
(2) For the ploughing and other tillage of land already under cultivation;
(3) For the hot weather deep ploughing of the black cotton soil.[3]

Indian farmers also are beginning to make great progress in the use of better implements. In many villages to-day we can see improved ploughs, hoes, water-lifts and other implements.[4] Likewise the use of plough is noticed in the Purāṇic days too. It is recommended in the Purāṇas that the ground for constructing a temple should be ploughed with white bullocks, that for a tank with bullocks of any colour, for preparing a garden and for digging well, black coloured bullocks are mentioned.[5] Hence, it can be known that the indigenous implements are still used today for the process of agriculture.

## 104. Part 17 - Classification of Soils


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Classification of Soils’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 17 - Classification of Soils

[Full title: Agriculture in the Modern Age of 20-21st Century (3) Classification of Soils]

The investigations of JW Leather (the first Imperial Chemist of Agricultural Research Institute, Pusa) in 1898, led to a classification of Indian soils into four major types:

(i) Indo-Gangetic alluvium;
(ii) black cotton or regur soils;
(iii) red soils lying on metamorphic rocks; and
(iv) laterite soils.[1]

In addition to the four principal groups of soils described above, mention may be made of four more groups.[2] They are:

(i) Forest and Hill Soils
(ii) Desert Soils
(iii) Saline and Alkaline Soils
(iv) Peaty and Marshy Soils

Various types of grounds are mentioned in the Purāṇas long ago. They are-white coloured ground, blood coloured ground, black clay and yellow coloured ground.[3] Again golden soil, rocky soil, blue soil, white soil and yellowish red soil are also mentioned.[4]

## 105. Part 18 - Sowing of Seeds


Agriculture and Animal husbandry in the Puranas

by Purboshri Borpuzari | 2019 | 74,328 words

This page relates ‘Sowing of Seeds’ of the study on agriculture and animal husbandry in the Puranas—a category of Sanskrit literature describing the culture and heritage of ancient Indian society. Besides detailing animal care, the six chapters of this essay also documents various Puranic agricultural science: ploughing, sowing, soil-types/fertility tests, transplantation, manuring, irrigation/water-management, tanks, rainfall–Sun dependence, horticulture, plus crop lists (e.g., Vrihi, Yava, Godhuma) and festivals/rites.

Go directly to: 
Footnotes.

Part 18 - Sowing of Seeds

[Full title: Agriculture in the Modern Age of 20-21st Century (4) Sowing of Seeds]

Sowing is one of the most important farm procedures. The germination of seeds, growth as well as the plant propagation depends on the time of sowing the seeds and the way in which it is done.

The seeds which are proposed for sowing should assure the following conditions:—

(1) They should belong to the proper variety or strain of the crop which is proposed to be grown;
(2) They should be clean and free from obvious mixtures of other seeds;
(3) They should be mature, well developed and plump in size;
(4) They should be free from obvious signs of age or bad storage such as mouldiness, insect attacks, bad smell, etc.;
(5) Above all they should have a high germinating capacity.[1]

The seeds of certain crops are subjected to some kinds of treatment before sowing, for different purposes, some of which are of a special character while others are ordinary.

These purposes comprise:

(1) convenience in sowing,
(2) the control of disease,
(3) protecting against white-ants,
(4) hastening or facilitating germination,
(5) inducing earliness,
(6) increasing the yield, and
(7) inducing variation in the progeny.[2]
